Java is a versatile and robust programming language that's highly popular among

developers for its platform independence and extensive ecosystem. It allows you to write code once and run it anywhere, thanks to the Java Virtual Machine (JVM).

History and Evolution

Java is a high-level, class-based, object-oriented programming language that was designed to have as few implementation dependencies as possible. It is intended to let application developers write once, run anywhere (WORA), meaning that compiled Java code can run on all platforms that support Java without the need for recompilation.

Origins of Java

The origins of Java date back to 1991 when it was initiated by James Gosling, Mike Sheridan, and Patrick Naughton, members of the "Green Team" at Sun Microsystems. The project was originally called "Oak," named after an oak tree that stood outside Gosling's office. However, due to trademark issues, it was later renamed "Java," inspired by Java coffee, which the developers consumed in large quantities.

Early Development

The initial goal of Java was to develop a language that could be used for digital devices such as set-top boxes and televisions. However, as the internet began to emerge as a significant force in computing, the focus shifted to leveraging Java for the web. The language's ability to produce dynamic, interactive content was a perfect match for the evolving World Wide Web.

Key Milestones

● 1995: Java 1.0 - Java was officially launched by Sun Microsystems. It quickly gained popularity due to its new features like platform independence and portability. The slogan "write once, run anywhere" highlighted its capability to run on any device equipped with the Java Virtual Machine (JVM).

● 1996: Java 1.1 - This version introduced inner classes, JavaBeans, JDBC (Java Database

Connectivity), and RMI (Remote Method Invocation). It also improved security features. ● 1997-1999: Java 2 (J2SE 1.2, 1.3, 1.4) - Java 2 introduced the Swing graphical API, Collections
Framework, and the HotSpot JVM, which significantly improved performance. This period also
saw the introduction of the Enterprise Edition (J2EE) and Micro Edition (J2ME) for enterprise
and mobile applications, respectively.
● 2004: Java 5 (1.5) - This version marked a significant update with features like generics,
metadata annotations, enumerated types, and the enhanced for loop. The introduction of the Java
Memory Model improved concurrency handling.
● 2006-2011: Java 6 and Java 7 - These versions focused on performance improvements and new
APIs. Java 6 enhanced web services support, while Java 7 introduced features like the try-withresources statement, the diamond operator, and the fork/join framework for parallel processing. ● 2014: Java 8 - A major milestone, Java 8 introduced lambda expressions, the Stream API, and
the new Date and Time API (java.time). These additions made Java more functional and
expressive, catering to modern programming paradigms.
● 2017: Java 9 - With the release of Java 9, the module system (Project Jigsaw) was introduced,
enabling better modularization and encapsulation of code. It also brought enhancements like
JShell, an interactive Java REPL, and improvements to the JVM.
● 2018 and Beyond: Java 10 to Java 17 - These releases continued to add new features and
enhancements. Java 10 introduced local variable type inference with the var keyword. Java 11
included long-term support (LTS) and added new APIs and improvements. Java 12 to Java 17
brought incremental improvements, such as switch expressions, text blocks, records, sealed
classes, pattern matching, and performance enhancements.
● 2023 :Java 21 LTS Release

Anticipated Features

● Pattern Matching Refinements
● Foreign Function & Memory API ● Scoped Values
● Vector API
● JVM Performance
● Language Features
● Developer Tooling
● Security and Stability
● Modern Technology Integration

These features will enhance Java for developers As of now the current version of Java LTS release is Java 21. For more info look below

The Open Source Journey

In 2006, Sun Microsystems made a big decision to make Java open-source. This means they allowed anyone to see and modify the Java code. They did this under a license called the GNU General Public License (GPL). This move led to the creation of the OpenJDK (Open Java Development Kit). OpenJDK is now the official version of Java that everyone uses as a reference.

Making Java open-source was a game-changer. It allowed a large community of developers from all over the world to contribute to Java's development. This community-driven approach has helped Java grow faster and become more robust, as more people work on fixing bugs, adding features, and improving the language.

Acquisitions and Current State

In 2010, Oracle Corporation bought Sun Microsystems, which meant Oracle took over the responsibility of managing Java. Since then, Oracle has continued to update and improve Java. They introduced a new release schedule, with a new version of Java coming out every six months. This regular release cycle ensures that Java gets new features and improvements at a steady pace.

Today, Java is still one of the most popular programming languages. It’s known for being stable, scalable, and having strong community support. Java is used in many areas, including:

● Web Development: Building dynamic websites and web applications. ● Mobile Applications: Developing apps for Android devices. ● Enterprise Systems: Creating large-scale business applications. ● Cloud Computing: Building and running applications on cloud platforms.

Summary

Java's journey from a small project at Sun Microsystems to a widely-used programming language shows how adaptable and relevant it remains. Its evolution mirrors the changes in technology and the ongoing efforts of the developer community to keep it modern and efficient. By learning Java, you're becoming part of a vast and enduring ecosystem that has proven its value over time and continues to drive innovation in software development.

As you start learning Java, you’ll be tapping into a language that has been built and refined by a dedicated community of developers. This community has ensured that Java remains a powerful tool for creating a wide variety of applications, making it a great language to learn and use for your projects.

How Java Has Dominated Over Other Programming Languages

Java has maintained a strong position in the programming world for several reasons. Here’s a breakdown of the key factors that have contributed to its dominance:
1. Platform Independence

● Write Once, Run Anywhere: Java's slogan, "Write Once, Run Anywhere," highlights its platform independence. Java programs can run on any device that has the Java Virtual Machine (JVM), making it highly portable.

● Cross-Platform Compatibility: This feature has made Java a preferred choice for developing applications that need to run on multiple operating systems without modification.
2. Robust Ecosystem and Libraries

● Extensive Standard Library: Java comes with a comprehensive standard library that supports a wide range of functionalities, from data structures to networking and GUI development.
● Mature Frameworks: Frameworks like Spring, Hibernate, and Struts provide powerful tools for building enterprise-level applications, simplifying development processes and reducing boilerplate code.

3. Performance and Scalability

● Efficient JVM: The JVM has been continuously optimized for performance and efficiency. Modern JVMs use Just-In-Time (JIT) compilation to convert bytecode to native machine code, enhancing execution speed.

● Scalability: Java's architecture and ecosystem support the development of highly scalable applications, making it suitable for large-scale enterprise systems.
4. Strong Community and Industry Support

● Active Community: Java has one of the largest and most active developer communities. This community contributes to continuous improvements, offers support, and creates a wealth of opensource tools and libraries.

● Corporate Backing: Initially supported by Sun Microsystems and later by Oracle, Java has had strong corporate backing that ensures regular updates, security patches, and a clear development roadmap.

5. Security Features

● Built-in Security: Java has several built-in security features, such as the bytecode verifier, sandboxing, and robust exception handling, which help in building secure applications.
● Security Updates: Regular updates from Oracle ensure that Java applications remain secure against vulnerabilities.

6. Versatility

● Wide Range of Applications: Java is used in various domains, including: ○ Web Applications: Servlets, JSP, and frameworks like Spring MVC. ○ Mobile Applications: Primary language for Android development.
○ Enterprise Applications: Enterprise JavaBeans (EJB), Java EE, and other enterprise

frameworks.
○ Embedded Systems: Java ME (Micro Edition) for embedded systems and IoT devices. ○ Cloud Computing: Strong integration with cloud platforms and microservices

architectures.
7. Educational Use

● Teaching Language: Many universities and educational institutions use Java to teach programming and computer science fundamentals. Its clear syntax and comprehensive library make it a good learning tool.

8. Continuous Evolution

● Regular Updates: The six-month release cycle introduced by Oracle ensures that Java continues to evolve with new features and improvements.
● Modern Language Features: Introduction of features like lambda expressions, the Stream API, and modules (Project Jigsaw) keeps Java modern and relevant.

9. Enterprise Adoption

● Widespread Enterprise Use: Many large enterprises have built their systems in Java due to its reliability, scalability, and performance. This widespread adoption reinforces its continued use and dominance.

● Long-Term Support (LTS) Releases: Java's LTS releases provide stability for enterprise applications, ensuring that businesses can rely on a stable platform for their critical systems.
Conclusion

Java’s dominance over other programming languages can be attributed to its platform independence, robust ecosystem, performance, strong community support, security features, versatility, and continuous evolution. These factors have made it a reliable and preferred choice for a wide range of applications, from enterprise systems to mobile apps, ensuring its lasting relevance in the programming world.

● High Demand: Java is one of the most sought-after programming skills by employers.

How Java Can Shape Your Career

Learning Java can significantly impact your software development career in several ways:

● Opens Doors to Opportunities: Java's widespread use across various industries translates to a wider range of job options. You could work on web applications, mobile apps, enterprise software, or even cloud-based systems.

● Boosts Your Skillset: Java provides a strong foundation in object-oriented programming principles, a fundamental concept in modern software development. Mastering these principles makes you a more well-rounded programmer.

● Enhances Problem-Solving Skills: Learning Java teaches you critical thinking and problem-solving approaches that are valuable assets in any technical career.
● Increases Employability: Having Java on your resume demonstrates your ability to work with a powerful and in-demand technology. This can significantly boost your resume's appeal to potential employers.
● Provides a Stepping Stone: Java can serve as a springboard to learn other programming languages. The knowledge and skills gained with Java can be applied to other objectoriented languages, making it easier to expand your programming expertise.

In conclusion, Java is a powerful and versatile language that offers a clear path to a successful career in software development. Its strong demand, vast applications, and supportive community make it an excellent choice for anyone looking to enter the exciting world of coding.

What is the LTS Release?

An LTS (Long-Term Support) release in the context of Java refers to a version of the Java Development Kit (JDK) that is designated for long-term support by the maintainers. LTS releases are intended to provide stability and support for a more extended period, making them suitable for applications and systems that require a predictable and well-supported platform.

Here are the key characteristics of Java LTS releases:

Extended Support Period: LTS releases receive updates and patches for an extended period compared to regular, non-LTS releases. This extended support period typically lasts for several years.
Predictable Maintenance Schedule: LTS releases follow a predictable schedule for updates and maintenance, which allows organizations to plan for and schedule necessary updates with confidence.

Stability and Compatibility : LTS releases prioritize stability and backward compatibility, making them a preferred choice for enterprises and projects that require a reliable and consistent development and runtime environment.

Recommended for Production Environments : LTS releases are generally recommended for use in production environments where long-term stability and support are crucial. Non-LTS releases, on the other hand, may be more suitable for developers who want to access the latest features and improvements.

Enterprise Adoption : Many large enterprises and organizations prefer to standardize their software development and deployment on LTS releases to minimize the risk of disruptions and ensure a wellsupported infrastructure.

Note: Java regular feature releases happen every 6 months, and LTS releases every 2 years.

Features of Java

Write Once, Run Anywhere (WORA):

You write your program once, and it can run on different types of devices and systems without major changes. It's like having a single recipe that works in different kitchens.

Once you write your code ,it can be executed anywhere on any platform.

Object-Oriented:

Java: The Object-Oriented Zoo!

Imagine a zoo, but instead of animals, we have objects in our Java programs. Object-oriented programming (OOP) is a way of thinking about programs like this zoo.

● Objects: These are the building blocks of your program, just like animals are the inhabitants of the zoo. An object can represent anything: a car, a bank account, even a song! Each object has its own unique characteristics.

● Properties: Think of these as the features of an object. For a car object, properties could be its color, speed, or number of doors. In our zoo, each animal might have properties like its species, age, or favorite food.

● Behaviors: These are the actions objects can perform. A car object might have behaviors like accelerate, brake, or turn. An animal object might have behaviors like eat, sleep, or make a sound.

Here's a simple example:
Imagine an object called "Dog" in your Java program. This Dog object could have properties like breed ("Golden Retriever"), age (3), and name ("Buddy"). It could also have behaviors like bark(), wagTail(), and fetch().
For developers familiar with programming basics, Java's object-oriented approach (OOP) offers a powerful way to structure and manage code.

Here's a breakdown from a programmer's perspective:
1. Objects: The Stars of the Show

● In Java, objects are the fundamental entities that encapsulate data (properties or attributes) and functionality (methods or behaviors). Imagine them as custom data types you define.

● A Car object, for example, might have properties like color, model, and horsepower. It could also have methods like accelerate(), brake(), and turn()
2. Classes: The Blueprints

● Classes act as blueprints or templates for creating objects. They define the properties and methods that all objects of a particular type will share.
● Think of a class as a factory that produces Car objects. You define the specifications (properties and methods) in the class, and then you can create multiple Car objects (instances) with those specifications.
3. Encapsulation: Keeping Things Private (and Secure!)
● Encapsulation is a key OOP principle. It allows you to control access to an object's properties. You can define some properties as private, meaning they can only be accessed by methods within the same object. This promotes data security and prevents accidental modification.
● In your Car class, you might make some properties like engineState private and provide methods like startEngine() and stopEngine() to control its state.
4. Inheritance: Borrowing Code for Efficiency
● Inheritance lets you create new classes (subclasses) that inherit properties and methods from existing classes (superclasses). This promotes code reuse and reduces redundancy.
● You could create a SportsCar class that inherits everything from the Car class but adds specific properties like the turboBoost() method.
5. Polymorphism: Making Objects Flexible
● Polymorphism allows objects of different classes to respond to the same method call in different ways. This adds flexibility and makes your code more adaptable.
● You could have a generic makeSound() method in your Car class that produces an engine sound. Inherited classes like SportsCar could override this method to create a more highperformance sound.

Platform Independence:

Java achieves platform independence through its bytecode and the Java Virtual Machine (JVM). When you compile a Java program, it gets translated into bytecode, which is a set of instructions for the JVM. The JVM then translates this bytecode into machine code that the specific operating system can understand and execute.

1. Java Bytecode:

○ When you write Java code, it’s compiled into something called bytecode (not machine-specific code).
○ Bytecode is like a universal language that any Java Virtual Machine (JVM) can understand.

2. JVM (Java Virtual Machine):

○ The JVM acts as an interpreter for bytecode.
○ It runs on different operating systems (like Windows, Linux, or macOS). ○ So, you write code once, and the JVM makes it work everywhere.

Example
○ Imagine you create a Java program (let’s call it HelloWorld.java).
public class HelloWorld {
public static void main(String[] args) { System.out.println("Hello, World!"); }
}

○ You compile it using javac HelloWorld.java. This generates HelloWorld.class (bytecode).
○ Now, you can run it on any OS with a JVM:
■ On Windows: java HelloWorld
■ On Linux: java HelloWorld
■ On macOS: java HelloWorld

That’s why Java is platform-independent—thanks to bytecode and the JVM!

Automatic memory management/Garbage collector

Automatic Memory Management in Java is like having a robot that cleans up your room after you finish playing. Here’s how it works:

Explanation

1. Memory Allocation: When you create objects (like variables, arrays, or instances of classes) in Java, they use up memory to store data.
2. Automatic Cleaning: Java’s automatic memory management, also known as garbage collection, is like having a robot that watches over your program. It cleans up (deallocates) memory used by objects that are no longer needed.
3. No Manual Cleanup: Just like you don’t need to manually clean up your room after playing, Java developers don’t have to manually free up memory. The garbage collector robot takes care of it.

Example

Imagine you create several objects in your Java program, like this:

How It Works

● Memory Allocation: Each time you create a variable (message, numbers) or an array (numbers), Java reserves memory to store their data.
● Automatic Cleanup: When these objects are no longer needed (for example, when they go out of scope or are no longer referenced), the garbage collector robot identifies them and frees up the memory they were using.

Benefits

● Prevents Memory Leaks: Ensures that unused memory is reclaimed, preventing memory leaks which can slow down programs over time.
● Simplifies Development: Developers can focus on writing code without worrying about manually managing memory, making Java programs more robust and reliable.

Real-World Analogy

● Robot Cleanup: Just like a robot that tidies up your room, Java’s garbage collector ensures that your program’s memory remains organized and efficient, even as it runs complex operations.

Summary

Let’s break down automatic memory management in a way that’s easy to understand: 1. Cleaning Up Like a Robot:

○ Imagine you have a robot friend who helps tidy up your room after you finish playing.
○ The robot picks up toys (objects) you’re no longer using and puts them away.
○ In Java, we have something similar called garbage collection (GC). 2. Java’s Garbage Collection (GC):
○ When you create objects (like toys) in Java, memory is allocated for them.
○ But what if you forget to clean up (delete) unused objects? That’s where GC comes in.
○ The GC checks which objects are no longer needed (like toys you’re not playing with).
○ It automatically frees up memory by removing those unused objects. 3. Example:
○ Suppose you create a bunch ofCar objects in your Java program.
○ When you’re done using them (no references to them), the GC identifies them as “garbage.”
○ It then cleans up by reclaiming the memory occupied by those unused Car objects.

So, Java’s automatic memory management is like having a helpful robot friend that keeps your computer’s memory tidy!

Multi-Threaded

Multithreading in Java allows your program to execute multiple tasks concurrently. It's like having different parts of your code run simultaneously, such as chatting with a user while downloading a file in the background.

1. What Is Multithreading?:
○ Imagine you’re cooking in the kitchen. You can do multiple things at once: ■ Stir the soup while chopping vegetables.
■ Check the oven while setting the table.

○ Similarly, multithreading in Java allows your program to handle different tasks simultaneously.

2. Java and Threads:
○ In Java, a thread is like a mini-worker within your program. ○ Each thread can do its job independently, like:

■ One thread handling user input (chatting).
■ Another thread downloading a file.

○ Threads run concurrently, so your program doesn’t get confused. 3. Benefits:
○ Faster execution: While one thread waits (e.g., downloading), others keep working.
○ Responsiveness: Your app can respond to user input even during heavy tasks.
4. Example:
○ Imagine a music player app:
■ One thread plays music.
■ Another thread updates the playlist.
■ Yet another thread shows album art.
○ All happening together, smoothly!

So, multi-threading is like having kitchen helpers—each doing their part without chaos! Feel free to add this explanation to your book! If you need more details or examples, just ask!

Security

In the world of programming, Java is like a superhero that protects your computer from bad things.
1. Java’s Shield:

○ Imagine your computer as a castle, and your program is the treasure inside.
○ Java comes with a built-in shield (like a superhero’s armor) to guard against villains (malicious code).
2. How It Works:
○ When you write Java code, it’s like building walls around your treasure.
○ Java checks every door and window (code) to make sure no sneaky villains (bugs or hackers) can get in.
○ If any bad code tries to break in, Java’s shield blocks it!
3. Superpowers:
○ No Memory Leaks: Java automatically cleans up after itself (like a tidy superhero).
○ No Unauthorized Access: Java controls who can enter (only trusted code).
○ No Evil Spells: Java checks for dangerous actions (like casting spells) and stops them.
4. Stay Safe!:
○ When you use Java, you’re like a castle owner with a superhero guard.
○ Your treasure (data) stays safe, and you can sleep peacefully at night!

standard library.

4. Variety of Dishes:

Data Type
Imagine you have a school locker where you keep different kinds of things: books, lunch, and gym clothes. Each of these things needs a specific type of container to fit properly. In Java, data types are like those containers. They help the computer know what kind of data you're storing and how much space it needs.

Here are some basic data types in Java:

➔ int: A box for whole numbers (without decimals), like 7, -3, or 100. ➔ double: A box for decimal numbers, like 3.14, 2.718, or -0.001. ➔ float: Another box for decimal numbers, but it takes up less space than a double. It's like double's little brother. ➔ long: A big box for very large whole numbers, like 123456789L. It can hold bigger numbers than int. ➔ short: A smaller box for whole numbers, like 10 or -5. It holds smaller numbers than int. ➔ byte: The smallest box for whole numbers, holding tiny numbers between -128 and 127. ➔ char: A box for single letters or symbols, like 'A', 'b', or '7'. ➔ boolean: A special box that can only hold true or false. It’s like a simple light switch that’s either on or off. In Java, data types are important because they define the kind of information you can store and work with.
● Primitive data types like integers, decimals, characters, and booleans are used for simple values. ● Non-primitive data types like arrays, classes, and interfaces are used for more complex structures and bigger sets of data.

Data types in Java determine how memory stores a variable's values. Each variable has a data type that specifies what kind of value it can hold. Primitive data types are also used to define the return type of functions.

int data type in Java is used to store integer values (whole numbers) that do not have any decimal places. Here are the key points about the int data type:
● Name: int (short for integer).
● Size: 32 bits (4 bytes).
● Range: From -2,147,483,648 to 2,147,483,647.
● Default Value: 0.
● Usage: Suitable for most integer calculations and storage in Java programs.

Example

I want to store the Age of the person whose age is 30 yrs. Int age=30;

Explanation:
Declaration and Initialization:
When you write int age = 30; you are performing two actions:
1. Declaration: You declare a variable named age of type int. This tells Java to allocate memory for storing an integer value. 2. Initialization: You initialize the variable age with the value 30. This means you assign the value 30 to the memory location reserved forage.
Memory Storage:
● Java allocates 32 bits (4 bytes) of memory for an int variable age. ● The value 30 is stored in this allocated memory space.
Explain howint age = 30; is getting stored in memory:

1. Memory Representation:
○ When you declareint age = 30;, Java allocates memory to store the value30. ○ Theint data type uses 4 bytes (32 bits) of memory.
○ The binary representation of30 is 00011110.

2. Visual Representation:
○ Here’s a simple diagram showing how it’s stored:
○
3. Explanation:

○ The variableage occupies 4 consecutive memory locations (bytes). ○ The binary representation of30 (in 8 bits) is padded with leading zeros to fit 32 bits. ○ So, age is stored as 00000000 00000000 00000000 00011110.

Remember, this memory layout ensures efficient storage and retrieval of data!

Access and Usage:
● Onceage is stored in memory, you can use it in your program for calculations, comparisons, or any other operations that involve integer values.
Example:
int age = 30;
● Here, age is declared as an int. ● Java reserves memory forage and assigns the binary representation of30 to that memory location.

2. //Add your methods
3. public static void main(String[] args) {
4. int age=30;
5. System.out.println("Your Age is : "+age);
6. }
7. }

Same like above, we can check other Data Types

double (double-precision floating-point)
● Used to store decimal numbers (more precise than float). 1. double pi = 3.14159; 2. System.out.println("Pi: " + pi); // Outputs: Pi: 3.14159
float (single-precision floating-point)
● Used to store decimal numbers (less precise than double). 1. float temperature = 36.6f; // The 'f' indicates that this is a float value. 2. System.out.println("Temperature: " + temperature); // 3. Outputs: Temperature: 36.6
long (long integer)
● Used to store large whole numbers. 1. long distance = 123456789L; // The 'L' indicates that this is a long value. 2. System.out.println("Distance: " + distance); // Outputs: Distance: 123456789
short (short integer)
● Used to store smaller whole numbers. 1. short height = 160; 2. System.out.println("Height: " + height); // Outputs: Height: 160
byte (byte value)
● Used to store very small whole numbers. 1. byte level = 12; 2. System.out.println("Level: " + level); // Outputs: Level: 12
char (character)
● Used to store a single character. 1. char grade = 'A'; 2. System.out.println("Grade: " + grade); // Outputs: Grade: A
boolean (boolean value)
● Used to store true or false values. 1. boolean isJavaFun = true; 2. System.out.println("Is Java Fun? " + isJavaFun); // Outputs: Is Java Fun? true
NOTE: We will look into Non-Primitive Data Type later, once we will have some basic knowledge of class and Objects.

What is Class ?

Think of a class in Java like a blueprint for making something. Let's say you want to make a toy robot. The blueprint (class) tells you what the robot looks like, what it can do (like walk or talk), and how it behaves. It's like having instructions to build and use the robot.

In Java, a class is where you define what an object can do and what information it stores. It's a way to organize your code neatly and create many objects that work in similar ways but might have different data inside them.

As you can see in the above image, BOX has various elements like Circle,Square,Rectangle and Triangle.All these Elements are of some TYPE and that TYPE is nothing but a Shape ,and we used to say this SHAPE as a Class.

In Java, a class is a blueprint or a template for creating objects. Objects are instances of classes, and a class defines the properties and behaviors that its objects will have.

Let's understand with another example.

We have 2 Objects, 1st one is Basic Phone and it is used to make calls.
And one is smartPhone, that has capability to make audio call ,video call,whatsapp call and also users can access the internet.

Each of these Objects has some properties like length,width,price ,name ets and both of these Objects/Phones has some actions.
These properties are called attributes in Object -oriented programming and Action is known as method.

Attributes tell some information about the Object where Method performs some action to do some task. In Java, a class serves as a blueprint or a template for creating objects. Imagine it like this: when you build a house, you follow a set of plans (blueprints) that describe how the house should be constructed. Similarly, a class defines the structure and behavior of objects.

Here are the key points about classes in Java:

1. Attributes (Properties): A class defines attributes (also known as properties or fields). These attributes represent the data associated with an object. For example, if we have a Car class, its attributes could include things like color, make, and model.

2. Methods (Behavior): A class also contains methods. Methods are like the actions that an object can perform. For instance, a Car class might have methods like startEngine() and brake(). These methods define what the car can do.

3. Creating Objects: Once you’ve defined a class, you can create objects from it. An object is an instance of a class. Going back to our Car example, you can create individual cars (objects) based on the Car class blueprint.

What is Object

Imagine you have a blueprint for making a toy robot. The blueprint (class) tells you how to build the robot, what it looks like, and what it can do. Now, when you build the robot following that blueprint, what you have is an object.

In Java, an object is like the actual toy robot you build from the blueprint (class). It's a real, tangible thing that has specific characteristics and can perform actions based on what the blueprint defines.

Key Points:

1. Blueprint (Class): Defines what an object will be and what it can do.
2. Characteristics: Objects have attributes (like color, size,price,shape OR any other properties)

and behaviors (like walk, talk,play or any activity).

3. Usage: Objects are used to represent real-world things or concepts in your Java programs.

Lets simplify it with Example:

Observing the below Image carefully, You can see that We have a BOX.This BOX is full of different kinds of Shapes like Circle,Square,Rectangle and Triangle.

An object is a basic unit of an object-oriented programming approach.

It is any real-world thing that has properties and actions.
Attributes (Properties):Objects have attributes that describe them.As you can see above Length,Width and Radius are properties to define the Object.

Methods (Action/Behaviors) :Objects can perform actions or behaviors. As you can see in the image below, the Circle can Roll and Rectangle can slide on the surface. This is nothing but the action that can be done with these Shape Objects and we used to call it a method in Java Programming.

Hope you are clear about class and object concepts.
Now we will move on to how to write code with class and method.

Variable

A variable is a container that holds a value or a reference to an object during execution. Variables are used to store and manipulate data within a program.

Each variable has a data type, which defines the kind of data it can hold (such as numbers, characters, or objects)
How to declare variable:
To create a class, you use the class keyword followed by the class name. Class names should always start with an uppercase letter.
For example:

Create Object for class Car :

To create an object (an instance of the Car class), use the new keyword followed by the class name and parentheses.
For example:

Car myCar = new Car();

Access Attributes and Methods: You can now access the attributes and methods of class Car using dot(.) operator.
myCar.color = "Red"; // Set the color myCar.startEngine(); // Start the engine

Method

Imagine you have a set of instructions to make a sandwich. You follow these instructions to make the sandwich whenever you want. In Java, a method is like a set of instructions that perform a specific task.

Key Points:

1. Definition: A method is a block of code that performs a particular task. It is defined within a class.
2. Purpose: Methods are used to organize code into reusable blocks, making programs easier to understand and maintain.
3. Components: A method has a name, a return type (what it gives back, if anything), parameters (inputs it can take), and a body (the instructions it executes).

Naming convention for Method:

Here are the naming conventions for methods in Java:

1. Method Names:
○ Should start with a lowercase letter.
○ Use camelCase for multi-word names, starting each subsequent word with a capital letter. ○ Example:calculateSum, printMessage, getUserInfo.

2. Avoid Underscores:
○ Use of underscores (_) in method names is discouraged unless following a widelyaccepted convention (likegetters and setters).
3. Verb Usage:

○ Method names should typically use verbs or verb phrases to describe actions performed by the method.
○ Example:calculate, print, validate.
4. Be Descriptive:
○ Choose meaningful names that clearly indicate the purpose of the method.
○ Avoid overly abbreviated or cryptic names.
○ Example: UsecalculateTotalPrice instead ofcalcPrice, if calculateTotalPrice accurately describes what the method does.

Syntax:
modifierreturn_typemethod_Name() // with args

Example:
public int getRollNo() ; // This Method will return some Value

modifierreturn_typemethod_Name() // with args

Example:
public void getRollNo() // will NOT return any value

modifierreturn_typemethod_Name(ParameterType1

parameterName1,ParameterType2 parameterName2 …)
Example:
Public int getSum(int a,int b)

What does the method do?
➔ Method can return some value to the caller ➔ Method may not return some value to caller ➔ Method can performs a specific task ➔ Methods can be called by any methods or code block.

Let's consider a use-case,As we know a method is a block of code that can perform some specific task as required.
Use-case: Write a method to add 2 numbers
Lets think , what we need if you are looking to ADD 2 numbers. Input: we need 2 numbers(Integer)
Let's assume , number1=10 and number2=20;
Lets see how we can declare and initialize the 2 variables which will hold these values.
int number1=10; int number2=20;
As we need to ADD 2 numbers and get the SUM as a result ,we will write a method that will return the SUM as an integer.
Method Body : without method arguments

public int getSumOfTwoNumbers(){ int number1=10;
int number2=20;
int sum=0;
sum=number1+number2;
return sum;
}

Method Body : with method arguments

public int getSumOfTwoNumbersWithArgs(int number1,int number2){ int sum=0;
sum=number1+number2;
return sum;
}

How to call Method?

static method : it can be called directly followed by class Name.
Example : class_Name.methodName(method arguments)

public class SumDemo {
public static int addTwoNumbers(){
System.out.println("addTwoNumbers method is called"); int number1=10;
int number2=20;
int sum=0;
sum=number1+number2;
return sum;

}
public static void main(String[] args) { System.out.println("Add two Numbers ##");
int sumOfNums=addTwoNumbers();
System.out.println("#### SUM :: "+sumOfNums); }
}
Result:

Add two Numbers ##
addTwoNumbers method is called. #### SUM :: 30

non-static method : it can be called directly followed by class Name.

sum=number1+number2;
return sum;

Type of Methods
Static Methods:
● Belong to the class, not to any specific instance. ● Can be called without creating an instance of the class. ● Can access only static variables and other static methods directly. ● Cannot access instance variables or instance methods directly.
class MathOperations {
// Static method static int add(int a, int b) { return a + b; } }

How to call a static method ?

Static Methods: Called on the class itself. Use ClassName.methodName().

Like :className.methodName()

Use-case 1: Write a method that returns some value.

public class MethodReturnValueDemo {

public int getNumber1(){ int num=10;
return num;

}

public int getNumber2(){ int num=10;
return num;

}

public int getNumber3(){ int num1=10;
int num2=10;
int sum=num1+num2; return sum;

}
public static void main(String[] args) {
MethodReturnValueDemo obj=new MethodReturnValueDemo();

int num1=obj.getNumber1(); int num2=obj.getNumber2(); int num3=obj.getNumber3();

System. out.println("Num 1 "+num1); System.out.println("Num 2 "+num2); System.out.println("Num 3 "+num3);

}
}
Result:

Num 1 : 10
Num 2 : 10
Num 3 : 20

Explanation:

This program demonstrates how to create and use methods that return values in Java. It defines a class MethodReturnValueDemo with three methods (getNumber1, getNumber2, and getNumber3) that return integer values. It then uses these methods in the main method to print the results.
Detailed Explanation
Class Definition
public class MethodReturnValueDemo {
● public class MethodReturnValueDemo: This line declares a public class named MethodReturnValueDemo. ● A class is a blueprint for creating objects.
Method Definitions
Method:getNumber1

public int getNumber1(){ int num = 10;
return num;

}

◆ public int getNumber1(): This line declares a public method named getNumber1 that returns an int. ◆ int num = 10;: Inside the method, a variable num is declared and assigned the value 10. ◆ return num;: The method returns the value of num, which is 10.
Method:getNumber2

public int getNumber2(){ int num = 10;
return num;

}

◆ This method is identical to getNumber1. It declares a variable num, assigns it the value 10, and returns10.
Method:getNumber3
public int getNumber3(){ int num1 = 10; int num2 = 10; int sum = num1 + num2; return sum; }
◆ public int getNumber3(): This line declares a public method named getNumber3 that returns an int. ◆ int num1 = 10;: A variable num1 is declared and assigned the value 10. ◆ int num2 = 10;: Another variable num2 is declared and assigned the value 10. ◆ int sum = num1 + num2;: A variable sum is declared and assigned the value of num1 plus num2, which is 20. ◆ return sum;: The method returns the value of sum, which is 20.
Main Method

public static void main(String[] args) {
MethodReturnValueDemo obj = new MethodReturnValueDemo(); }

● public static void main(String[] args): This line declares the main method, which is the entry point of the program. ● MethodReturnValueDemo obj = new MethodReturnValueDemo(); ● This line creates an objectobj of the classMethodReturnValueDemo. Calling Methods and Printing Results

int num1 = obj.getNumber1(); int num2 = obj.getNumber2();

int num3 = obj.getNumber3();
int num1 = obj.getNumber1();
● Calls the getNumber1 method using the obj object and stores the returned value (10) in the variable num1.
int num2 = obj.getNumber2();
● Calls the getNumber2 method using the obj object and stores the returned value (10) in the variable num2.
int num3 = obj.getNumber3();
● Calls the getNumber3 method using the obj object and stores the returned value (20) in the variable num3. These lines print the values ofnum1, num2, and num3 to the console. ● System.out.println("Num 1 " + num1);: Prints Num 1 10. ● System.out.println("Num 2 " + num2);: Prints Num 2 10. ● System.out.println("Num 3 " + num3);: Prints Num 3 20. Summary
1. Class: MethodReturnValueDemo defines methods to return integer values. 2. Methods: ○ getNumber1: Returns10. ○ getNumber2: Returns10. ○ getNumber3: Returns the sum of10 and 10 (i.e., 20). 3. Main Method: Creates an object of the class and calls the methods to print their return values.

Calling the Method

What is a Constructor ?

➔ A constructor is a special type of method that is used to initialize objects.

➔ It is called when an object is created using thenew keyword.

➔ The primary purpose of a constructor is to set the initial state of an object by assigning values to its instance variables or performing other necessary setup tasks.

Key Points About Constructors
1. Same Name as Class: A constructor has the same name as the class. 2. No Return Type: Unlike regular methods, a constructor does not have a return type (not even void). 3. Called Automatically: A constructor is automatically called when an object of the class is created.

Why Do We Need Constructors?

Imagine you're building a toy car. When you take it out of the box, you might need to put the wheels on and add batteries before you can play with it. The constructor is like the setup step that gets the toy car ready for use.
Example of a constructor

public class Employee {
//constructor
public Employee() {
//you can add initialization code here }
}

public class Employee { String name;
int employeeId;

public Employee(String name, int employeeId) { this.name = name;
this.employeeId = employeeId;

}
}
Let's say we have a class Car.

public class Car { String color; int speed;
// Constructor public Car(String color, int speed) { this.color = color; this.speed = speed; }
// Method to display car details public void displayDetails() { System.out.println("Color: " + color); System.out.println("Speed: " + speed + " km/h"); }
public static void main(String[] args) { // Creating an object of Car and calling the constructor Car myCar = new Car("Red", 120);
// Displaying the details of the car myCar.displayDetails(); } }
Class Definition:
◆ public class Car: Defines a class named Car.
Constructor:
public Car(String color, int speed): ◆ This is the constructor. It takes two parameters, color and speed. this.color = color; ◆ Sets the instance variable color to the value passed in. this.speed = speed; ◆ Sets the instance variable speed to the value passed in.
Creating an Object:
◆ Car myCar = new Car("Red", 120); ◆ This line creates a new Car object called myCar. ◆ The constructor is called with the arguments "Red" and 120.
Displaying Details:
◆ myCar.displayDetails(); ◆ Call the displayDetails method on myCar to print its details.
When you run the program, it will output:

Color: Red
Speed: 120 km/h

Summary:
● A constructor is a special method used to initialize objects. ● It has the same name as the class and no return type. ● It's automatically called when an object is created. ● It helps set up the initial state of the object.

public class ConstructorDemo { String name;
int employeeId;
double salary;

public ConstructorDemo() { }

public ConstructorDemo(String name) { this.name = name;
}

public ConstructorDemo(int employeeId) { this.employeeId = employeeId;
}

public ConstructorDemo(double salary) { this.salary = salary;
}

public ConstructorDemo(String name, int employeeId) { this.name = name;
this.employeeId = employeeId;

}
public ConstructorDemo(int employeeId, double salary) { this.employeeId = employeeId;
this.salary = salary;
}

public ConstructorDemo(String name, double salary) {
this.name = name;
this.salary = salary;

}
public ConstructorDemo(String name, int employeeId, double salary) { this.name = name;
this.employeeId = employeeId;
this.salary = salary;
System.out.println(name+" ,"+employeeId+", "+salary); }

public static void main(String[] args) {
System.out.println("#create Object with no-arg"); ConstructorDemo defaultObj=new ConstructorDemo();
System.out.println("#create Object with one arg"); ConstructorDemo attrName=new ConstructorDemo("Raj");
System.out.println("#create Object with 2 args"); ConstructorDemo nameAndId=new ConstructorDemo("Raj",12);
System.out.println("#create Object with 3 args");
ConstructorDemo nameAndIdAndSal=new ConstructorDemo("Raj",12,22111d); }
}

Abstract Class
➔ An abstract class is a class that cannot be instantiated on its own and is meant to be subclassed by other classes. ➔ Abstract class is a class that may contain abstract methods as well as concrete methods.
➔ Concrete method is just a simple method with implementation.implementation may be default or empty.
➔ Abstract method : it's a method without implementation.
➔ An abstract method is a method that is declared in an abstract class or interface but does not have any implementation in that class or interface.
➔ Abstract methods are meant to be implemented by the subclasses or classes that implement the interface.
➔ Abstract methods are declared using the abstract keyword.

They don't have a body (implementation).
public abstract class Animal {
// Abstract method declaration

public abstract void makeSound();

}

➔ Abstract methods do not provide any implementation in the abstract class or interface that declares them. ➔ ➔ The purpose is to provide a common method signature that must be implemented by concrete subclasses or implementing classes.

public abstract void makeSound();

As you can see, the method makeSound() does not have any implementation. Main purpose of abstract methods is to create a method with signature and make it available for the users/client so that client can provide the implementation of these methods as per their requirements.
While exposing abstract methods to clients we expect them to provide the implementation where the access modifier and return type of the method will be applicable to the implemented method.
public abstract int addTwoNumbers(int number1,int number2);
As we have the method ,addTwoNumbers(int number1,int number2) which will simply ADD Two numbers- number1 and number2 which are of type integer.

public abstract class CalculatorDemo {
// Abstract method declaration

public abstract int addTwoNumbers(int number1,int number2);

}

As you can see ,in the above code we have abstract class CalculatorDemo and it has one abstract method addTwoNumbers(int number1,int number2) which has 2 arguments of type integer.This method does not have any implementation for this method.means the we have NOT added any logic to ADD Two numbers in this method, we have just defined the rule that this method will ADD Two numbers and will return the result of type integer.
As we have created this method for the client and we expect them to provide its implementation as per their requirements and method should return the result of type integer BUT we are NOT putting any restriction on how to write the logic to ADD Two numbers. If any class will extend this class CalculatorDemo, then this class needs to provide the implementation for the method addTwoNumbers(int number1,int number2) OR this class should be declared as an “abstract class”.
If you are new to Java ,then you might have a question like ,-if we are NOT providing implementation then what is the meaning of creating this method?
In Simple words we can say that I want someone to provide this implementation as per their requirement.Here I am just a Authority who is exposing this service to client and setting some rules like how this method will be accessible and what value it should return.it really help to make your code flexible and reusable.
Interface
In java, Interface is a way to enable us to design multiple inheritance.As we know multiple inheritance is NOT supported in Java but the same is possible using interface.
Interface is one of the best ways to expose your service to clients ,it provides one level of abstraction in java.
It cant be instantiated like an abstract method.
Before java 8 , we can have only abstract methods in interface BUT in later versions of java after java 8 , you can have default methods and static methods also.
In simple words , -you can say that an interface is a way to expose your services without showing background details.
Interfaces provide a level of abstraction by defining a set of methods without specifying their implementations.
Classes implementing the interface must provide concrete implementations for all the abstract methods.
Naming Convention for Interface
● Interfaces should be named using PascalCasing, with the first letter of each word capitalized.
● It should be meaningful, instead of acronyms
● It should be an adjective such as Runnable, Remote, ActionListener.

Syntax:
interface Interface_Name
Example:
interface EmployeeService interface StudentService How to declare interface:

public interface Interface_Name {

//any abstract/static/private/default method }
Example:
public interface StudentService {
public abstract double getStudentMarks(int studentId); public abstract String getStudentAddress(int studentId); }
We can have static method,private method ,default method and abstract method in interface.all these methods has its own purposes.being a developer you need to know its use.
Lets create an Interface with all these method combination
public interface OrganizationService {

//Abstract method
public abstract String getEmployeeData(int employeeID); public double getEmployeeSalary(int employeeId);

//default method ,if you want to add some default implementation for your method default String getOrgName(){
return "XYZ pvt ltd";
}
//adding static method,
public static int getOrgId(){ return 234;
}
//adding private method ,for internal usage private String getOrgKey(){
return "secret-abc";
}
}
Some key info about Abstract class
1. If a class has any abstract method then the class must be an abstract class BUT if a class is an abstract class then it's NOT necessary that the class will have an abstract method.

2. Abstract class can not be instantiated.means Object cant be created for that class.
3. If we don't want someone to create an object for your class BUt you just wanted to expose your services/methods to client THEN its good practice to declare that class as abstract.

4. If you extend some abstract class THEN it's not necessary that you have to implement its abstract method.if you don't want to implement some abstract method then just declare your class as abstract.

Let's create one abstract class EmployeeService and add some abstract methods to it as below.
Use-case 1: extend above abstract class EmployeeService and don't provide implementation for abstract method.
As we have already seen that we need to provide implementation for abstract methods OR we need to declare the class as abstract.
Here We have declared the class as abstract.
Use-case 2: extend the abstract class by child class and implement its abstract methods.

public class EmployeeServiceHelper extends EmployeeService{
@Override
public double getEmployeeSalary(int employeeId) {

//You can ADD your own implementation
if(employeeId==1){
return 10000;
}
if(employeeId==2){
return 20000;
}
else {
return 30000;
} }

@Override
public String getEmployeeAddress(int employeeId) { //You can ADD your own implementation
if(employeeId==1){
return "Raj";
}
if(employeeId==2){
return "Syam";
}
else {
return "Rohan";
}
}
}

Use-case 3: try to create instance/object of abstract class

Why can't we create objects of an abstract class ?

In Java, you cannot create an object of an abstract class directly because abstract classes may have abstract methods without implementations. An abstract class is meant to be a base class or a blueprint for other classes, and it often contains incomplete or abstract methods that need to be implemented by its subclasses.

I ncomplete Implementation:
Abstract classes may have one or more abstract methods, which are methods declared without a body. Since abstract methods lack an implementation in the abstract class itself, it would be inappropriate to create an object of the abstract class because the object would be incomplete or lack certain functionalities.

Enforcing Subclass Implementation:
Abstract classes are designed to be extended by other classes.
The abstract class provides a common structure, including abstract methods, and expects its subclasses to provide the specific implementations for those abstract methods.
Creating an instance of an abstract class without concrete implementations for its abstract methods would violate the contract defined by the abstract class.

Abstract Classes as Blueprints:
Abstract classes often serve as blueprints or templates for other classes.
They encapsulate common functionality and provide a basis for customization in subclasses. Since abstract classes may lack complete implementations, they are not suitable for direct instantiation. public abstract class Animal {

// Abstract method
public abstract void makeSound();
// Concrete method
public void sleep() {
System.out.println("### sleep now."); }
}

public class Dog extends Animal {
// Implementation of the abstract method @Override
public void makeSound() {

System.out.println("#### bhou bhou .."); }
}

public class Main {
public static void main(String[] args) {
// Cannot create an object of an abstract class
// Animal animal = new Animal(); // This line would result in a compilation error

// Create an object of a concrete subclass
Animal dog = new Dog();
dog.makeSound(); // Calls the overridden method in the Dog class dog.sleep(); // Calls the method from the abstract class

}
}

In this example, Animal is an abstract class with an abstract method makeSound(). You can create an object of the concrete subclass Dog and use it polymorphically through the Animal type. This ensures that the abstract method is implemented in the subclass.

Access Modifier

access modifiers are keywords that define the visibility or accessibility of classes, methods, and fields within a program. These modifiers control which other classes or parts of the program can access the declared elements.

Java has four main access modifiers:

public

The public modifier makes a class, method, or field accessible from any other class. It has the widest scope and allows unrestricted access.

public class MyClass {
public int publicVariable; public void publicMethod() {

// Code here
}
}
In the example above, publicVariable and publicMethod can be accessed from any other class.

Private (private)

The private modifier limits the visibility of a class member to only within its own class. It provides the most restricted access.

public class MyClass {
private int privateVariable; private void privateMethod() {

// Code here
}
}
Here, privateVariable and privateMethod can only be accessed within the MyClass itself.

Protected (protected)

The protected modifier allows access to the member within its own package and by subclasses (even if they are in a different package).

public class MyClass {
protected int protectedVariable; protected void protectedMethod() {

// Code here
}
}
protectedVariable and protectedMethod can be accessed by classes in the same package and by subclasses, whether in the same package or a different one.

Default (Package-Private, No Modifier)

If no access modifier is specified (default or package-private), the element is accessible within its own package but not from outside the package.

class MyClass {
int defaultVariable; void defaultMethod() {

// Code here
}
}
Here, defaultVariable and defaultMethod can be accessed by classes within the same package, but not by classes in different packages.

It's important to choose the appropriate access modifier based on the desired level of encapsulation and security for your program. Using access modifiers helps in enforcing encapsulation, promoting code reusability, and preventing unauthorized access to certain parts of the code.

Static keyword

static keyword is used to declare members (variables, methods, and nested classes)

that belong to the class itself rather than instances (objects) of the class.
Here’s howstatic works for variables and methods:

Static Variables

● Definition: When a variable is declared as static, it means there is exactly one copy of that variable shared among all instances (objects) of the class.
● Access: Static variables are accessed using the class name itself, rather than through instances of the class.
● Initialization: Static variables are initialized only once, at the start of the execution, before any object of the class is created.
● Scope: They exist for the entire duration of the program.
● Usage: Commonly used for constants (final static) or variables that need to be shared across all instances of a class.

public class Example { // Static variable static int count = 0;

public Example() {
count++; // Incrementing count in the constructor
}

public static void main(String[] args) { Example obj1 = new Example(); Example obj2 = new Example(); Example obj3 = new Example();

System.out.println("Number of instances created: " + Example.count); // Output: 3 }
}

Static Methods
➔ Definition: When a method is declared as static, it means the method belongs to the class rather than any specific instance. It can be called without creating an instance of the class. ➔ Access: Static methods can directly access other static methods and static variables of the class. ➔ Non-static Context: They cannot directly access instance variables or instance methods because they are not tied to any specific instance. ➔ Utility Methods: Often used for utility functions that do not require access to instance-specific data.
public class MathUtils {
// Static method to calculate factorial public static int factorial(int n) { if (n == 0 || n == 1) { return 1; } return n * factorial(n - 1); }
public static void main(String[] args) { int num = 5; int fact = MathUtils.factorial(num); System.out.println("Factorial of " + num + " is: " + fact); // Output: 120 } }
OOPS concepts

Object-Oriented Programming (OOP) is a programming paradigm that uses objects – instances of classes
– to organize and structure code. Java is an object-oriented programming language, and it supports key OOP concepts.

Here are the main OOP concepts in Java:
Class and Object
class :A class is a blueprint or template for creating objects. It defines the properties (attributes) and behaviors (methods) that objects of the class will have.

public class CarDemo { // Class attributes (fields) String model;
int year;

// Class methods
void startEngine() {
// Code to start the engine }
}
Object: An object is an instance of a class. It represents a real-world entity and can have its own unique state and behavior.
Suppose we have a class Student where we have attributes model and year.

this.name = name;

Encapsulation

Encapsulation is the bundling of data (attributes) and methods that operate on the data into a single unit (class).
Encapsulation is a way to wrap up variables(data) and methods together as a class.
As you can see in the image below,this capsule contains multiple ingredients and all these ingredients form a capsule that help us to recover from the given disease.
In java ,class also contains different attributes and methods that help us to perform some task.here entire class works like a capsule and do its job.
Encapsulation is just the way to encapsulate the attributes and methods together in a class. public class BankAccount {
private double balance; // Encapsulated attribute // Public method to access the balance public double getBalance() {
return balance;
}
// Public method to deposit money public void deposit(double amount) { balance += amount;
}
}

Some info about Inheritance

Inheritance is a mechanism that allows a class (subclass/derived class) to inherit the properties and behaviors of another class (superclass/base class).
Inheritance is a mechanism where one class acquires the property of another class.
For example, a child inherits the traits of his/her parents.
With inheritance, we can reuse the fields and methods of the existing class. Hence, inheritance facilitates Reusability and is an important concept of OOPs.
It promotes code reusability and establishes a relationship between classes.

While designing software,separation of concern is one of the most important aspects. We used to keep common attributes and methods in the parent class and other classes will just inherit that common behavior.This is nothing but an inheritance.

In Java, when an “Is -A” relationship exists between two classes, we use Inheritance. The parent class is called a super class and the inherited class is called a subclass. The keyword extends is used by the sub class to inherit the features of super class.

Type of inheritance

Single Inheritance: one class extends another class (one class only)
As you can see above, Class B extends only one Class A. Class A is a super class and Class B is a Sub-class.
Here class B is inheriting the behavior from its parent class A.

Multiple Inheritance:

one class extending more than one class. Note: Java does not support multiple inheritance.

As you can see above, Class C is inheriting the behavior from TWO classes Class A and Class B,This is NOT supported in Java.One class can extend only one class but it can implement any number of Inheritance. We will look into it in detail later in this section.

Multilevel Inheritance

one class can inherit from a derived class. Hence, the derived class becomes the base class for the new class.

As you can see here, A class can inherit behavior from other classes at different levels.we used to have such inheritance when we were looking for hierarchy in our classes where one class will extend only one class.

As you can see here,Class B inherits behavior from class A and class C inherits behavior from class B and you can ADD even more as per your requirements.we will keep adding the different behavior to that at different levels and child class will have all the behavior available in all the parent classes.

In the above example, Class B will inherit behavior from class A and Class C will inherit behavior from Class B and Class A.

Hierarchical Inheritance

One class is inherited by many other subclasses.
As you can see here,Shape is the parent class here and it has 3 child classes Triangle,Circle and Rectangle.
Lets understand Inheritance with Examples.
public interface OrganizationService {
public abstract String getEmployeeData(int employeeID);
public double getEmployeeSalary(int employeeId);
//default method ,if you want to add some default implementation for your method default String getOrgName(){
return "XYZ pvt ltd";
}
//adding static method,
public static int getOrgId(){
return 234;
}
//adding private method ,for internal usage
private String getOrgKey(){
return "secret-abc"; }
}
Create an object of OrganizationServiceImpl and call the methods. public class OrganizationDemo {

String orgName= organizationService.getOrgName(); String empData= organizationService.getEmployeeData(1); double salary=organizationService.getEmployeeSalary(2);

}

XYZ pvt ltd Raj
10000.0

Abstraction

Abstraction in Java is a concept used to simplify complex systems by modeling classes appropriate to the problem, and by working with these models rather than the details of implementation. It's like focusing on what an object does instead of how it does it. This helps in managing complexity by hiding the unnecessary details from the user and only exposing the essential features.

Imagine you’re using a smartphone. You know that pressing the call button will let you make a phone call, but you don’t need to know how the phone internally connects to the network, processes the signals, and so on. The complexity is hidden from you, and you only see the necessary features that allow you to make a call. This is an abstraction.

Key Points of Abstraction in Java

1. Hiding Implementation Details:

○ You use methods to perform actions without knowing the inner workings of those methods.
○ Only the essential details are visible, while the internal complexities are hidden. 2. Abstract Classes and Interfaces:
○ Java provides two ways to achieve abstraction: abstract classes and interfaces.

Abstraction in Java helps in managing complexity by exposing only the necessary features of an object and hiding the implementation details. It is achieved using abstract classes and interfaces, allowing you to focus on what an object does rather than how it does it. This concept makes your code cleaner, more modular, and easier to maintain.

Abstract Classes

An abstract class is a class that cannot be instantiated on its own and is meant to be subclassed by other classes.
We have already discussed it in detail earlier in this section.
Example:

public abstract class Shape {
// Abstract method (to be implemented by subclasses) public abstract double calculateArea();

// Concrete method public void display() { System.out.println("This is a shape."); }
}

Abstract Methods

Abstract methods are declared in abstract classes or interfaces and do not provide an implementation in the declaring class or interface.
They define a contract that concrete subclasses or implementing classes must fulfill by providing their own implementations.

Interfaces

Interfaces are another way to achieve abstraction in Java. They consist of abstract methods (and, starting from Java 8, default and static methods).
A class can implement multiple interfaces, allowing it to inherit multiple sets of abstract methods.

Example:

public interface Sound { // Abstract method void makeSound();

}

Hiding Implementation Details

Abstraction helps in hiding the internal details of how a class or method achieves its functionality. It allows users of a class or interface to interact with the essential features without needing to know the intricacies of the implementation.

Focus on Relevant Details

Abstraction enables developers to focus on the relevant details of a class or system, ignoring unnecessary complexities.
This makes the code more modular, maintainable, and easier to understand.

Method Signature
Method signatures consist of method name and argument type.

● return type is NOT a part of method signature.
● Two methods with the same signature will NOT be allowed in the same class in Java.

Polymorphism

Polymorphisms are derived from 2 Greek words: poly and morphs. The word "poly"

means many and "morphs" means forms. So polymorphism means many forms.

Polymorphism in Java is a concept by which we can perform a single action in different ways.
It refers to the ability of a class to take on multiple forms.
There are two types of polymorphism in Java: compile-time (or static) polymorphism and runtime (or dynamic) polymorphism.

Compile-time Polymorphism (Method Overloading)

➔ Method overloading allows a class to have multiple methods with the same name but different parameters (different types or a different number of parameters).
➔ The compiler determines which method to call based on the method signature during compiletime.
➔ If a class has multiple methods having the same name but different in parameters, it is known as

Method Overloading.

➔ If we have to perform only one operation, having the same name of the methods increases the readability of the program.
➔ In java,multiple methods can have the same name and return type with different numbers OR different type of method arguments.

Let's assume, you want to perform addition of numbers, so you can have some sample methods as below.
Some example of method overloading: With different type of parameters:

public int printNumbers(int number1) public int printNumbers(float number1) public int printNumbers(double number1) public int printNumbers(long number1)

public int addNumbers(int number1,int number2) public int addNumbers(float number1,int number2) public int addNumbers(float number1,float number2) public int addNumbers(long number1,long number2) public int addNumbers(double number1,double number2)

With different number of parameters:
public int addNumbers(int number1,int number2,int number3) public int addNumbers(int number1,int number2,int number3,int number4)

Example:

public class NumbersCalculatorDemo {
public long addNumbers(int number1,int number2){ int sum=number1+number2;
return sum;

}

public long addNumbers(long number1,long number2){ long sum=number1+number2;
return sum;

}

public long addNumbers(long number1,long number2,long number3){ long sum=number1+number2+number3;
return sum;

}
public static void main(String[] args) {
NumbersCalculatorDemo calculatorDemo=new NumbersCalculatorDemo();

long sum1= calculatorDemo.addNumbers(10,20); long sum2= calculatorDemo.addNumbers(10l,20l); long sum3= calculatorDemo.addNumbers(10,20,30);

System. out.println("## Sum 1:"+sum1); System.out.println("## Sum 2:"+sum2); System.out.println("## Sum 3:"+sum3);

} }
Result:

## Sum 1:30
## Sum 2:30
## Sum 3:60

Example 2:
Main purpose of method overloading is to make your services more flexible so that clients can use your services/methods with different possible method arguments.
Same method will be called by clients using different possible method arguments.it will help clients to have better experience.
When we are going to expose our services to any third party clients ,we used to provide overloaded methods to the client for better experience.
Even Java also uses this feature for inbuilt methods.
As we can see here, println(..) is a method in the PrintStream class in java that is used to print values to the console.
We can see that this method is an overloaded method and it is expecting different type of method arguments.

For more info,please look into the code below.
We have a method method1(..) which is an overloaded method and accepts arguments like int,float,double and long.

When some will call this method, based on the provided value data type it will call the method. Here overloadingDemo.method1(11f), we are providing float value while calling this method ,the compiler will detect it and it will call the method with the same argument type.

public void method1(float number1){
System.out.println("#### method1 with arg type : float"); }
public class MethodOverloadingDemo {
public void method1(int number1){

System. out.println("#### method1 with arg type : int"); }
public void method1(float number1){

System.out.println("#### method1 with arg type : float"); }
public void method1(double number1){
System.out.println("#### method1 with arg type : double"); }
public void method1(long number1){
System.out.println("#### method1 with arg type : long"); }
public static void main(String[] args) {
MethodOverloadingDemo overloadingDemo=new MethodOverloadingDemo();

overloadingDemo.method1( 11); overloadingDemo.method1(11f); overloadingDemo.method1(11d); overloadingDemo.method1(11l);

}
}
Now what if the compiler didn't get a method with the same provided type?
Automatic promotion in overloading:
In overloading,method resolution is always taken care by compiler based on reference type hence overloading also considered as compile time polymorphism or static binding or early binding.

While resolving overloaded methods, if an exact match is NOT found we won't get any compile time error immediately.first compiler promotes the method argument to next level and checks for the method match and it will keep checking to the highest level possible.

If method match found then compiler will consider it and it will make the call to this method otherwise compiler promotes the method argument(s) to next level this process will be continue until the possible promotion and if match is still not found then it will throw compile time error.this is called automatic promotion in overloading.

Example:
public class MethodOverloadingDemo {
public void method1(double number1){
System.out.println("#### method1 with arg type : double"); }
public void method1(long number1){
System.out.println("#### method1 with arg type : long");
}
public static void main(String[] args) {
MethodOverloadingDemo overloadingDemo=new MethodOverloadingDemo();
overloadingDemo.method1(11);
}
}

Runtime Polymorphism (Method Overriding)

Whatever the methods the parent has,by default it will be available to its child classes.
If a child is NOt satisfied with parent class method implementation,then child is allowed to provide its own implementation to that method as per their requirement, this process is called method overriding.
Parent class method which is overridden is called the overridden method and the child class method that is overriding the existing parent behavior is called the overriding method.
Method overriding occurs when a subclass provides a specific implementation for a method that is already defined in its superclass. This allows a subclass to provide a specialized version of a method that it inherits from its superclass.
Example:
class Animal {
void makeSound() {
System.out.println("Generic animal sound");
}
void makeFight() {
System.out.println("Animal is fighting");
}
}
class Dog extends Animal {
// Method Overriding
void makeSound() {
System.out.println("Bark");
}
}

class Cat extends Animal {
// Method Overriding
void makeSound() {

System.out.println("Meow");
}

}
In this example, both Dog and Cat classes override the makeSound method from the Animal class. The specific version of the method is determined during runtime based on the actual object type.

public class AnimalDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {

Animal animal1= new Animal(); Animal animal2=new Dog(); Animal animal3=new Cat();

animal1.makeSound(); animal2.makeSound(); animal3.makeSound();

} }
Result:

Generic animal sound Bark
Meow

Explanation:
public class AnimalDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {

Animal animal1= new Animal(); Animal animal2=new Dog(); Animal animal3=new Cat();

animal1.makeSound(); animal2.makeSound(); animal3.makeSound();

}
}

Runtime polymorphism work on runtime object so
Animal animal2=new Dog(), mean it will call the method from Dog NOT from Animal
class.

Explanation
Key facts:
In overriding,method resolution is always taken care of by JVM based on Runtime objects hence overriding is also called Runtime polymorphism OR dynamic Polymorphism OR Late binding.
Overriding method resolution is also known as Dynamic method dispatch.
In Overloading,method resolution is based on reference type BUT not based on Runtime object where in method overriding method resolution is always taken care by JVM based on Runtime object.
In method overriding,method name and arguments must be matched.means method signature must be the same.
Private methods will NOT be visible in child class,the overriding concept is NOT applicable for private methods.
In overriding, we can't reduce the scope of the access modifier.
If the child class method throws any checked Exception,then the parent class method should throw the same Exception or its parent otherwise we will get a compile time Exception.
In overriding, we can't reduce the scope of the access modifier.
If the child class method throws any checked Exception,then the parent class method should throw the same Exception or its parent otherwise we will get a compile time Exception.

Data Hiding

Static method cant be overridden , because method overriding is Runtime Polymorphism which depends on objects BUT static method does not affect objects at class level.

static methods cannot be overridden in the same way that instance methods can be. When a subclass defines a static method with the same signature as a static method in its superclass, it is considered hiding, not overriding. This is due to the fundamental differences between static and instance methods.

Here are the key reasons why static methods cannot be overridden in Java:

Inheritance vs. Hiding

Inheritance involves creating a new class that is a modified version of an existing class, and overriding allows a subclass to provide a specific implementation for a method that is already defined in its superclass.

Static methods, on the other hand, are associated with the class itself, not with instances of the class. When a subclass declares a static method with the same signature as a static method in its superclass, it is considered hiding the superclass method, not overriding it.

Class vs. Instance Context

Static methods belong to the class rather than to instances of the class. They are invoked using the class name, not through an instance of the class.
Instance methods, which can be overridden, are associated with instances of a class and are called on an object.

Resolution at Compile-Time

The resolution of static methods is done at compile-time, not runtime. This means that the method to be called is determined based on the declared type of the reference variable, not the actual type of the object during runtime.

In contrast, instance methods are resolved at runtime, allowing for dynamic method dispatch.

Control statement

Control statements in Java are constructs that manage the flow of execution in a program. They enable you to control the order in which statements are executed based on certain conditions or criteria.
Java provides several types of control statements:

1. if
2. while
3. do-while
4. for
5. forEach
6. break
7. continue
8. switch

Control Statements can be divided into three categories, namely

● Selection statements ● Iteration statements ● Jump statements

In Java , we have these statements to control the flow.Based on our need we can have these statements anywhere in code.

If Statement

Theif statement in Java is a conditional statement that allows you to execute a block of code if a specified condition evaluates to true

Assuming that you are going to buy a mobile ,Shopkeeper asked you if you give me money
, i will give you a mobile.

Note-If you will NOT give money shopkeeper will NOT give you mobile.
Syntax:

if(condition) {
// Statements to execute if // condition is true
}

package org.example.ifStatement;
public class SimpleIfStatementDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {
int number=2;
if(number==2){
System.out.println("This is 2"); }
}
}
Result:
This is 2

System.out.println("This number is EVEN and Smaller than 10"); }
else if(number > 10) {
System.out.println("This number is EVEN and Greater than 10"); }
else if (number > 100) {
System.out.println("This number is EVEN and Greater than 100"); }
else{ System.out.println("## default :: without any condition"); }
}
}
