result.forEach(x-> System.out.println(x));

If you will use supplyAsync(..) and runAsync(..) without Executor Service then CompletableFuture will use ForkJoinPool to create no of Threads and that will be by default same as no of available processors.
Walkthrough of Other Important methods of CompletableFuture
CompletableFuture_Intance.thenXXX(Functional Interface Instance)
Example:
CompletableFuture_Instance.thenApply(Function_Instance)
Here Function is a Functional Interface that takes an input and returns another input. we can use it for data manipulation.
Add “Mr.” to the name of each Employee in async.

Code walkthrough CompletableFuture_Instance.thenApply(Function_Instance)

CompletableFuture<List<String>> currentWorkingEmpData=CompletableFuture .supplyAsync(()-> EmplUtil.getAllCurrentEmployeesData()
.stream()
.map(EmployeeData::getName)
.collect(Collectors.toList()));

CompletableFuture<List<String>> listCompletableFuture=currentWorkingEmpData .thenApply(x->x.stream()
.map(y -> "Mr. " + y)
.collect(Collectors.toList()));

List<String> names=listCompletableFuture.get();
System.out.println(names);
CompletableFuture_Instance.thenAccept(Consumer_Instance)

CompletableFuture<List<String>> currentWorkingEmpData=CompletableFuture .supplyAsync(()-> EmplUtil.getAllCurrentEmployeesData()
.stream()
.map(EmployeeData::getName)
.collect(Collectors.toList()));

currentWorkingEmpData.thenAccept(x->{
System.out.println(x);
}); //you can call any Consumer as part of thenAccept method arg
Just like the above example, we can use any Functional Interface method to execute the chain of CompletableFuture to execute in Async mode.
Combining 2 CompletableFuture results:
You can use any of the below methods as per requirements.

Methods :
thenCompose(Function<? super T,? extends CompletionStage<U>> fn)
Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this stage completes normally, is executed with this stage as the argument to the supplied function.

thenComposeAsync (Function<? super T,? extends CompletionStage<U>> fn)
Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this stage completes normally, is executed using this stage's default asynchronous execution facility, with this stage as the argument to the supplied function.

thenComposeAsync (Function<? super T,? extends CompletionStage<U>> fn, Executor executor) Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this stage completes normally, is executed using the supplied Executor, with this stage's result as the argument to the supplied function.

CompletableFuture<List<EmployeeData>> listCompletableFuture2=listCompletableFuture1 .thenCompose(s->CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(()->Java8Demo.getOldEmpList(s)));
System.out.println(listCompletableFuture1.get());

Here both CompletableFuture returns the same type of object. If you want to combine 2 completely different Future.

thenCombine (CompletionStage<? extends U> other, BiFunction<? super T,? super U,? extends V> fn) Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this and the other given stage both complete normally, is executed with the two results as arguments to the supplied function.

thenCombineAsync (CompletionStage<? extends U> other, BiFunction<? super T,? super U,? extends V> fn)
Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this and the other given stage complete normally, is executed using this stage's default asynchronous execution facility, with the two results as arguments to the supplied function.

thenCombineAsync (CompletionStage<? extends U> other, BiFunction<? super T,? super U,? extends V> fn, Executor executor)
Returns a new CompletionStage that, when this and the other given stage complete normally, is executed using the supplied executor, with the two results as arguments to the supplied function.

CompletableFuture<String> completableFuture = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "RAMA") .thenCombine(CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(

() -> " KRISHNA"), (s1, s2) -> s1 + s2)); Result: RAMA KRISHNA
Here 2nd arg is BiFunction, BiFunction is Functional interface when takes 2 input and returns output.
You can use Consumers if you don't want to return anything.

CompletableFuture<String> completableFuture
= CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "RAMA") .thenCombine(CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(

() -> " KRISHNA"), (s1, s2) ->System.out.println(s1+s2)));
Result: RAMA KRISHNA ,same result..but no return value.we are directly Printing using BiConsumer functional interface.
Executing multiple Futures together:

CompletableFuture<String> completableFuture1
= CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "RAM");
CompletableFuture<String> completableFuture2
= CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "SYAM");
CompletableFuture<Void>
finalResult=CompletableFuture.allOf(completableFuture1,completableFuture2); System.out.println(finalResult.get());//NULL ,because finalResult is not combined result //We need to get it manually as below
System.out.println(completableFuture1.get()); //RAM
System.out.println(completableFuture2.get()); //SYAM

But if your requirements to get joined result then, String result= Stream.of(completableFuture1,completableFuture2) .map(CompletableFuture::join)
.collect(Collectors.joining(" "));

System.out.println(result); //RAM SYAM

Lombok library in java

Let's look into some out of box features that we can use in java code while working on real time coding.

These features really help you to improve your skill.
Lombok is highly recommended to use real time projects in industry.you can use it to generate getter ,setter,constructors and many more.

Lombok is a Java library that provides annotations that help developers reduce boilerplate code. Lombok annotations can generate code such as getters and setters, constructors, and logging statements.

Some common Lombok Annotations: @Setter
@Getter
@Data
@NoArgsConstructor
@AllArgsConstructor
@ToString
@EqualsAndHashCode
@Slf4j
@Builder(toBuilder = true)

Add below maven dependency to your project class path(pom.xml)
@Getter :
it is used to create a getter method. It can be used at class level as well as at attribute level.
Without Lombok:With Lombok:

public class EmployeeRequest { String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
public String getEmpName() {

return empName;
}
public String getEmpEmail() { return empEmail;
}
public Long getEmpId() { return empId;}}

import lombok.Getter;
@Getter
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
At Attribute Level:
public class EmployeeRequest {

@Getter
String empName; @Getter
String empEmail; @Getter
Long empId;

}
@Setter :
it will create a setter method for class attributes.its applicable at class level as well as attribute level.
Without Lombok:
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
public void setEmpName(String empName) {

this .empName = empName;
}
public void setEmpEmail(String empEmail) {

this .empEmail = empEmail;
}
public void setEmpId(Long empId) {

this.empId = empId;
}
}
With Lombok:

import lombok.Getter;
@Setter
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
With getter and Setter Together:

import lombok.Getter;
import lombok.Setter;
@Getter
@Setter
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}
It will create a getter() and setter(..) method for all the class attributes.
If you want to customize the behavior ,like you want to create a getter/setter for a given attribute only THEN apply these annotations at field level.
@Data: this annotation is a combination of @Getter and @Setter BUT it is applicable at class level ONLY.

import lombok.Data;
@Data
public class EmployeeRequest {

String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;

}

}

@Data
public class EmployeeRequest {
String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
@Override
public boolean equals(Object o) {
if (this == o) return true;
if (o == null || getClass() != o.getClass()) return false;
EmployeeRequest that = (EmployeeRequest) o;
return empName.equals(that.empName) && empEmail.equals(that.empEmail) && empId.equals(that.empId);
}
@Override
public int hashCode() {
return Objects.hash(empName, empEmail, empId);
}
}

@Builder :

it will create a mutable instance of the annotated class. It will be applicable at class level as well as at method level. This annotation can be applied at :
I. Class Level
Ii. Method Level
iii. Constructor level

@Builder annotation uses Builder Pattern and helps us to create immutable instances of annotated class. If we apply at class level , by default it will consider all the fields for creating Objects.
I. Class Level

@Builder (toBuilder = true) public class EmployeeRequest { String empName;
String empEmail;
Long empId;
}

.empName("Raj")
.empEmail("raj@gmail.com")
.build();

}
}

employeeRequest.setEmpEmail("Raj@gmail");

@Synchronized:
This annotation is used on methods to make Java methods synchronized. This is for thread safety.

@Synchronized
public String getName(){
System.out.println("====== test ======");
return "Raj";
}

@NonNull:

This annotation on the parameter of a constructor or a method generates a null check. If put on a parameter, lombok will insert a null-check at the start of the method / constructor's body, throwing a NullPointerException with the parameter's name as a message. If put on a field, any generated method assigning a value to this field will also produce these null-checks.

@NonNull
@Builder(toBuilder = true)
public EmployeeRequest getClassInstance(String empName, String empEmail) {

EmployeeRequest employeeRequest= new EmployeeRequest();
employeeRequest.setEmpName(empName);
employeeRequest.setEmpEmail(empEmail);
return employeeRequest;

}

What is new in Java 21

Virtual Thread
Being a Java Developer , You must know what is Thread?

A Thread is a light-weighted process, or we can say the smallest part of the process that allows a program to execute that task.
Threads can be used to perform complicated tasks in the background without interrupting the main program.
Let's assume , I have to print “Welcome” 1 times, I will simply create a Thread and Thread will take care of printing the String “Welcome” 1 times as required.
In Java, We can create a Thread either by extending Thread class or by implementing Runnable Interface.
Hope you already know this.
Create Thread by Extending Thread class: public class MyThreadDemo extends Thread{
public void run() {
System.out.println("Welcome"); }
public static void main(String[] args) {
MyThreadDemo th=new MyThreadDemo(); th.start();
}
}
Result:
This code is running in a thread
Create Thread with Runnable:
public class ThreadWithRunnableDemo implements Runnable {
@Override
public void run() {
System.out.println("Welcome"); }
public static void main(String[] args) {
// Create Object of ThreadWithRunnableDemo
ThreadWithRunnableDemo threadWithRunnableDemo=new ThreadWithRunnableDemo(); //Create Thread
Thread th=new Thread(threadWithRunnableDemo);
//Start Thread th.start(); }
}
Result: Welcome
We can simplify the process of Thread creation using Lambda expression: public class ThreadWithLambdaDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {
//implement run() method of Runnable
Runnable th=()->System.out.println("Welcome");
//start Thread
new Thread(th).start();

//OR
Thread thread=new Thread(()-> System.out.println("Welcome")); thread.start();

}}
Result: Welcome

More about the Virtual Thread

As of Now we have seen that , We can create a Thread either by extending Thread class OR by implementing Runnable interface and we can even use Lambda Expression to simplify the code.

But our intention is NOT to just create a Thread ,creating a Thread in Java consumes some resources and memory from the system.

Thread can either of below type:

●OS Thread
●Platform Thread
●Virtual Threads

java.lang.Thread , Thread created using Thread class or Runnable interface are Platform Thread.
These Platform threads are typically mapped 1:1 to kernel threads scheduled by the OS.
OS threads are quite “heavy”. That makes them suitable for executing all types of tasks.
Depending on the OS and configuration, they consume somewhere between 2 and 10 MB by default.

Normally making requests to the server, each request consumes a Thread,So if you want to utilize a million requests/threads in your heavy-load concurrent application, you better have more than 2 TB of memory to spare.

As you can see that, creating too many platform Thread is NOT the better option since it will consume huge resources and slow down the system because OS Threads are limited, any system will NOT allow you to create more that limited Platform Threads because each Platform Thread is mapped with OS Thread.

So creating more Platform Thread than a limited number is just like waiting for OS Thread to be available so that Platform Thread can be mapped with OS Thread.

In Java, We used to have a Thread pool and we can simply pool Threads from the pool based on our requirements BUT as we know this has also a limitation on how many Platform Threads can be created.

What will happen if you create more Platform Threads than the allowed limit ? In simple words we can say that it will create Performance issues,deadlock,starvation, memory issues or even a system can be crashed.
Suppose an application with an average latency of 50ms achieves a throughput of 200 requests per second by processing 10 requests concurrently.

In order for that application to scale to a throughput of 2000 requests per second, it will need to process 100 requests concurrently. If each request is handled in a thread for the request's duration then, for the application to keep up and working , the number of threads must grow as throughput grows.

Unfortunately, the number of available threads is limited because the JDK implements threads as wrappers around operating system (OS) threads. OS threads are costly, so we cannot have too many of them, which makes the implementation ill-suited to the thread-per-request style.

If each request consumes a thread, and thus an OS thread, for its duration, then the number of threads often becomes the limiting factor long before other resources, such as CPU or network connections, are exhausted.

The JDK's current implementation of threads caps the application's throughput to a level well below what the hardware can support. This happens even when threads are pooled, since pooling helps avoid the high cost of starting a new thread but does not increase the total number of threads.

=> THis is where Virtual Thread comes into picture.

=>Here we will create Virtual Threads instead of Platform Thread.

Now The question is , how Virtual Threads and Platform Threads differ. Suppose a System has capacity to handle 500 Threads for the given throughput and given duration, what if you need 700 Threads to handle the in-coming requests and expect the same throughput at the same time ? In such case operating systems give the illusion of plentiful memory by mapping a large virtual address space to a limited amount of physical RAM, a Java runtime can give the illusion of plentiful threads by mapping a large number of virtual threads to a small number of OS threads.

Virtual Thread and Platform Threads are instances of java.lang.Thread

Virtual Thread not tied to a particular OS thread directly.
A platform thread is implemented in the traditional way, as a thin wrapper around an OS thread.
Virtual Thread is cheap and lightweight so JVM creates it instead of pooling it from Thread pool.
Virtual threads are a lightweight implementation of threads that is provided by the JDK rather than the OS.

Virtual threads employ M:N scheduling, where a large number (M) of virtual threads is scheduled to run on a smaller number (N) of OS threads.

Today, every instance of java.lang.Thread in the JDK is a platform thread. A platform thread runs Java code on an underlying OS thread and captures the OS thread for the code's entire lifetime. The number of platform threads is limited to the number of OS threads.

A virtual thread is also an instance of java.lang.Thread that runs Java code on an underlying OS thread but does not capture the OS thread for the code's entire lifetime. This means that many virtual threads can run their Java code on the same OS thread.

While a platform thread monopolizes a precious OS thread, a virtual thread does not.
Since Virtual Threads are lightweight, the number of virtual threads can be much larger than the number of OS threads for the same system configuration.

we can create a very high number of virtual threads ( millions) in an application without depending on the number of platform threads. These virtual threads are managed by JVM , lightweight in nature and stored in RAM as normal Java objects.

Key points:
1. Virtual Threads are always demon threads in nature.
2. Virtual Thread always has medium priority and it can't be changed.
3. Virtual Threads are lightweight and short lived.
4. Virtual Threads are NOT active members of Thread groups.
5. Virtual threads do not support the stop(), suspend(), or resume() methods.
6. Virtual Threads are NOT directly connected with OS Thread.
7. One Platform Thread can be mapped with many Virtual Threads.
8. Virtual threads are suitable for executing tasks that spend most of the time blocked, often waiting for I/O operations to complete.
9. Virtual threads are not intended for long running CPU intensive operations.

Create Virtual Thread

There are multiple ways to create Virtual Thread,we can create usingThread class OR Thread.Builder.
//Runnable reference
Runnable runnable = ()-> System.out.println("Welcome"); // Start a virtual thread to run a task

Thread thread = Thread.ofVirtual().start(runnable);

Example:
public class VirtualThreadDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception{
Thread thread = Thread.ofVirtual().start(()-> System.out.println("Hello"));

//Wait for Virtual Thread to finish. thread.join();
}

}
Result: Hello
OR
Runnable runnable = () -> {
try {

Thread. sleep(Duration.ofSeconds(1));
} catch(Exception e) {
System.out.println(e);
}
System.out.println("Work Done -"+ atomicInteger.incrementAndGet()); };

We can use the Thread.Builder reference to create and start multiple threads.

Runnable runnable = () -> System. out.println("Inside Runnable"); Thread.Builder builder = Thread.ofVirtual().name("JVM-Thread"); Thread thread1 = builder.start(runnable);
Thread thread2 = builder.start(runnable);

Thread thread3 = Thread.startVirtualThread(runnable);

Result:
Inside Runnable Inside Runnable Inside Runnable

Similarly Thread.ofPlatform() exists for creating platform threads as well.
Runnable runnable = () -> System.out.println("Welcome");
Thread.Builder builder = Thread.ofPlatform().name("Platform-Thread-demo");
Thread t1 = builder.start(runnable);
Thread t2 = builder.start(runnable);

Result : Welcome Welcome

Create unstarted Virtual Thread and start it explicitly:
public class VirtualThreadsDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {
Runnable runnable=()->System.out.println("Thread is running:Welcome");

Thread vThread = Thread. ofVirtual().unstarted(runnable); System.out.println("Is Virtual : "+(vThread.isVirtual())); System.out.println("Is Alive : "+(vThread.isAlive())); System.out.println("Is Daemon : "+(vThread.isDaemon())); System.out.println("Is Interrupted : "+(vThread.isInterrupted()));

System.out.println("########### starting Virtual Thread explicitly"); vThread.start();

System. out.println("Is Alive : "+(vThread.isAlive())); System.out.println("Interrupt the Thread "); //vThread.interrupt();

}
}

Result :
Is Virtual : true
Is Alive : false
Is Daemon : true
Is Interrupted : false
########### starting Virtual Thread explicitly Is Alive : true
Interrupt the Thread
Thread is running:Welcome

Compare Virtual Threads and Platform Threads

import java.time.Duration;
import java.time.Instant;
import java.util.concurrent.Executors;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger;

public class VirtuaThANdPlatformThDemo { public static void main(String[] args) {
final AtomicInteger atomicInteger = new AtomicInteger(); Runnable runnable = () -> {
try {

Thread. sleep(Duration.ofSeconds(1));
} catch(Exception e) {
System.out.println(e);
}
System.out.println("Welcome -"+ atomicInteger.incrementAndGet()); };

Instant start = Instant.now();
try (var executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(100)) {

for (int i = 0; i < 10_000; i++) {
executor.submit(runnable);
}
}

Instant finish = Instant. now();
long timeElapsed = Duration.between(start, finish).toMillis(); System.out.println("Total Elapsed Time : " + timeElapsed);

}
}
Result:

Welcome - 1
Welcome - 2
Welcome - 3
.
.
.
Welcome - 9999
Welcome - 9990
Total Elapsed Time : 100300 ms [//Approx 100 seconds]

Explanation:

As we have created 10,000 threads from this Runnable and executed them with virtual threads and platform threads to compare the performance of both. We will use the Duration.between() api to measure the total time in executing all the tasks.

First, we are using a pool of 100 platform threads. In this way, Executor will be able to run 100 tasks at a time and other tasks will need to wait. As we have 10,000 tasks, the total time to finish the execution will be approximately 100300 ms i.e 100 seconds.

Now we will create Virtual Threads so replace the Executors.newFixedThreadPool(100) with Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor(). This will execute all the tasks in virtual threads instead of platform threads.

Now you will be able to see the difference between Virtual Threads and Platform Threads.
As we have to wait for each call , for such use-cases it is recommended to use Virtual Threads instead of Platform Threads.

import java.time.Duration;
import java.time.Instant;
import java.util.concurrent.Executors;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger;

VirtuaThANdPlatformThDemo2 {public class
public static void main(String[] args) {
final AtomicInteger atomicInteger = new AtomicInteger(); Runnable runnable = () -> {
try {

Thread. sleep(Duration.ofSeconds(1));
} catch(Exception e) {
System.out.println(e);
}
System.out.println("Welcome - " +atomicInteger.incrementAndGet()); };

Instant start = Instant.now();
try (var executor=Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) { for(int i = 0; i < 10_000; i++) {
executor.submit(runnable);
}
}

Instant finish = Instant. now();
long timeElapsed = Duration.between(start, finish).toMillis(); System.out.println("Total elapsed time : " + timeElapsed);

}
}

Welcome - 2
Welcome - 3

.

Welcome - 9999
Welcome - 10000
Total elapsed time : 1313 ms [//Approx 1.5 seconds]

As you can see in the above code, it's just the difference of creating Virtual Thread OR Platform Thread.
var executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(100);
var executor = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor();
Explanation:

As you can see the total time taken to execute 10,000 times, Platform Thread is taking 100 seconds while Virtual Threads take on 1.5 seconds. It's around 70 times faster with the same throughput.

NOTE - As you can see the above example, Virtual Threads executed the job 70 times faster than the Platform Threads BUT it doesn't mean that Virtual Threads are FASTER than Platform Thread.

Virtual Threads are just the way to get higher throughput on the same system configuration at the same time. Let's increase the Virtual Thread count to 700,000 instead of 10,000 and see how much time it's taking on the same system.

As you can see in the example below, 700,000 virtual threads are taking just 10 seconds to execute all the jobs.

Join the Virtual Thread

Virtual thread runs always as a daemon thread.
the main thread is not blocked when a given task is executed by a virtual thread.

We can call thejoin() method to wait for the virtual thread to terminate.

There are multiple overload methods for join() method.you can use as per your preference.

join() //waits indefinitely
join(Duration duration) //wait in a time-bound manner join(long millis)

import java.time.Duration;

Deep dive:

Developers can choose whether to use virtual threads or platform threads. Here is an example program that creates a large number of virtual threads. The program first obtains anExecutorService that will create a new virtual thread for each submitted task.

It then submits 10,000 tasks and waits for all of them to complete:
try (var executor = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {

IntStream.range(0, 10_000).forEach(i -> {
executor.submit(() -> {
Thread.sleep(Duration.ofSeconds(1));
return i;

});
});
}

The task in this example is simple code — sleep for one second — and modern hardware can easily support 10,000 virtual threads running such code concurrently. Behind the scenes, the JDK runs the code on a small number of OS threads, perhaps as few as one.

We are using an ExecutorService that obtains platform threads from a pool, such as Executors.newFixedThreadPool(200). TheExecutorService would create 200 platform threads to be shared by all 10,000 tasks, so many of the tasks would run sequentially rather than concurrently and the program would take a long time to complete.

For this program, a pool with 200 platform threads can only achieve a throughput of 200 tasks-per-second, whereas virtual threads achieve a throughput of about 10,000 tasks-persecond (after sufficient warmup).

As we have changed the Thread count to 700,000 so the program will submit 700,000 tasks and all will run concurrently.Before using Virtual Threads, We should be knowing where to use Virtual Threads.
Suppose your task is to sort an array and its taking 1sec so it doesn't matter whether its Platform Thread OR Virtual Threads.Virtual Threads are mostly applicable to use where there is waiting/sleep kind of task is involved.

Virtual threads are not faster threads— they do not run code any faster than platform threads. They exist to provide scale (higher throughput), not speed (lower latency). Virtual Threads are mainly useful when :
■ The number of concurrent tasks is high
■ The workload is not CPU-bound, since having many more threads than processor cores cannot improve throughput in that case.

Virtual Threads with Web Application/Microservices

As we have gone through, how does Virtual Thread work ? Let's assume we have a Web Application that receives around millions of requests per second to fetch some data from remote services.if we prefer to opt thread-per-request model than we need around 1 million threads to process all the request, creating 1 million Threads in any application is costly process so its recommended to create Virtual Threads to process the request in this case and many WEB framework are evening supporting it.

our code will look something like below.
Java structured concurrency provides multiple options to take care of such use cases.

How to observe Virtual Threads

Just writing a clean executable code for multithreaded environments is not enough ,we should also think about troubleshooting, maintenance, and optimization.
Java does provide various tools and mechanisms to track Treads, Java debugger needed to get Thread call stacks, and inspect variables in stack frames.

JDK Flight Recorder (JFR), which is the JDK's low-overhead profiling and monitoring mechanism, can associate events from application code with the correct virtual thread. These tools cannot do these things for applications written in the asynchronous style. In that style tasks are not related to threads, so debuggers cannot display or manipulate the state of a task, and profilers cannot tell how much time a task spends waiting for I/O.

Traditional Java Thread dump is another option for troubleshooting in case of Threadper-request BUT its not enough in case of Virtual Threads because we can have millions of threads running concurrently.

Java has introduced a new kind of thread dump injcmd to present virtual threads

alongside platform threads, all grouped in a meaningful way.
JSON format of thread dump can be get using below command: $ jcmd <pid> Thread.dump_to_file -format=json <file>

How does Virtual Thread works internally

Any thread executes some task once it's scheduled for execution on a processor core, in case of Platform Thread which is a wrapper of OS Thread, JDK relies on the scheduler in the OS which used to take care of Thread scheduling.

In-case of Virtual Thread,JDK has its own scheduler. Instead of assigning virtual threads to processors directly, the JDK's scheduler assigns virtual threads to platform threads in M:N ration ,where multiple Virtual Threads can be assigned to a single Platform Thread.

The JDK's virtual thread scheduler is a work-stealing ForkJoinPool that operates in FIFO mode. The parallelism of the scheduler is the number of platform threads available for the purpose of scheduling virtual threads. By default it is equal to the number of available processors, but it can be tuned with the system property

jdk.virtualThreadScheduler.parallelism.

To run code in a virtual thread, the JDK's virtual thread scheduler assigns the virtual thread for execution on a platform thread by mounting the virtual thread on a platform thread. This makes the platform thread become the carrier of the virtual thread. Later, after running some code, the virtual thread can unmount from its carrier. At that point the platform thread is free so the scheduler can mount a different virtual thread on it, thereby making it a carrier again.

As you can see here,JVM Scheduler schedules Platform Threads with Virtual Threads and OS Scheduler takes care of scheduling OS Thread with Platform Thread

Sequenced Collections

Before moving forward for Sequenced Collection ,Lets understand collection and its class/interface hierarchy as per java 21 API docs.

The root interface in the collection hierarchy is Collection Interface.

A collection represents a group of objects. Some collections allow duplicate elements and others do not. Some are ordered, and others are unordered.
Some new interfaces have been introduced as part of java 21 that allow the existing collection classes to have a defined encounter order.
The order will have a well-defined first element, the second element, and so forth, up to the last element.
These new interfaces are:

● SequencedCollection
● SequencedSet
● SequencedMap

Collections that have a defined encounter order are generally subtypes of the SequencedCollection interface.

Why do we need SequencedCollection ?

Before Java 21, Java collections classes had no handy method to get first,last element,or adding element at first and last position as well as getting the reversed order of collection using predefined methods.

Being a developer , we used to do all this stuff manually as per our requirements but now as part of Java 21,this issue has been resolved.
Sequenced Collection is A collection that has a well-defined encounter order and supports operations at both ends, and that is reversible.
SequencedCollection is an Interface introduced as part of Java 21, similar to that SequencedMap and SequencedSet were introduced.
Interface declaration: Methods of Sequenced Collection:

SequencedMap

Like SequencedSet, SequencedMap contains the characteristics of SequencedCollection and Map.

● A SequencedMap provides methods to add, retrieve and remove elements at either end of the map.
● This interface also defines the reversed() method, which provides a reverse-ordered view of this map.
● A SequencedMap has a well-defined encounter order that supports operations at both ends and is reversible.
● A map's reverse-ordered view is generally not serializable, even if the original map is serializable.
● The encounter order of a SequencedMap is similar to that of the elements of a SequencedCollection, but the ordering applies to mappings instead of individual elements.

Interface Declaration as per Java 21

Structured Concurrency

Structured Concurrency, introduced in Java 19 as an incubator feature and in Java 21 as a preview feature, is a concept that improves the implementation, readability, and maintainability of code for dividing a task into subtasks and processing them concurrently.

Structured Concurrency is the way to execute multiple subtasks as part of the same task concurrently.
Structured concurrency treats groups of related tasks running in different threads as a single unit of work.

structured concurrency aims to simplify Java concurrent programs by treating multiple threads/sub-tasks as a single unit of work.These multiple threads are forked from the same parent thread, thus treated as a single unit of task.

structured concurrency allows developers to structure a task as a family of concurrent subtasks, and to coordinate them as a unit. Subtasks are executed in their own threads by forking them individually and then joining them as a unit and, possibly, canceling them as a unit. The subtasks' successful results or exceptions are aggregated and handled by the parent task.

import java.util.concurrent.Callable;
import java.util.concurrent.StructuredTaskScope;
public class StructuredConcurrencyDemo {

public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception { Callable<String> task1 = ()-> "Welcome";
Callable<Integer> task2 = ()-> 12;

try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope.ShutdownOnFailure()) {
StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<String> subtask1=scope.fork(task1); StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<Integer> subtask2=scope.fork(task2);

scope .join()// combine both tasks
.throwIfFailed();// throw error if any of task fails

var msg=subtask1.get(); var num1=subtask2.get();

String data= combineResponse(msg,num1); System.out.println("Data :: "+data); }
}

public static String combineResponse(String msg,Integer num){ return msg+" "+num;
}
}
Result:
Data :: Welcome 12
As you can see in the above example,We have 2 subtasks and we are combining both using join() method.
If any of the jobs fail then it will throw an exception.

StructuredTaskScope
The principal class of the structured concurrency API is StructuredTaskScope in the

java.util.concurrent package. This class allows developers to structure a task as a family of concurrent subtasks, and to coordinate them as a unit.

StructuredTaskScope supports cases where a task splits into several concurrent subtasks, and where the subtasks must complete before the main task continues. A StructuredTaskScope can be used to ensure that the lifetime of a concurrent operation is confined by a syntax block.

Subtasks are executed in their own threads by forking them individually and then joining them as a unit and, possibly, canceling them as a unit.
The subtasks' successful results or exceptions are aggregated and handled by the parent task.

StructuredTaskScope confines the lifetimes of the subtasks to a clear lexical scope in which all of a task's interactions with its subtasks— forking, joining, canceling, handling errors, and composing results— takes place.

Here is thehandle() example from earlier, written to useStructuredTaskScope (ShutdownOnFailure is explained below):
We can achieve below key benefits while using Structured Concurrency.

■ Error handling with short-circuiting — If either thefindUser() or fetchOrder() subtasks fail, the other is canceled if it has not yet completed. (This is managed by the shutdown policy implemented byShutdownOnFailure; other policies are possible).

■ Cancellation propagation — If the thread running handle() is interrupted before or during the call tojoin(), both subtasks are cancelled automatically when the thread exits the scope.

■ Clarity — The above code has a clear structure: Set up the subtasks, wait for them to either complete or be cancelled, and then decide whether to succeed (and process the results of the child tasks, which are already finished) or fail (and the subtasks are already finished, so there is nothing more to clean up).

■ Observability — A thread dump, as described below, clearly displays the task hierarchy, with the threads running findUser() and fetchOrder() shown as children of the scope.

Example:
import java.util.concurrent.Callable;
import java.util.concurrent.StructuredTaskScope;
public class StructuredConcurrencyDemo {

public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception { Callable<String> task1 = ()-> "Welcome";
Callable<Integer> task2 = ()-> 12;

try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope<Object>()) {
StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<String> subtask1 = scope.fork(task1); StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<Integer> subtask2 = scope.fork(task2);

scope.join();
//process results/exceptions }
}
}

As you can see, AStructuredTaskScope is created with one of its public constructors as you can see below.
It defines thefork(..) method to start a thread to execute a subtask.
It calls the join() method to wait for all subtasks to finish.
You can call close method to close the task scope But The API is intended to be used with thetry-with-resources statement.

The intention is that code in the try block uses thefork method to fork threads to execute the subtasks, wait for the subtasks to finish with thejoin method, and then process the results.

To understand it better, let's look at the example below.

Here we have 4 tasks that we wanted to RUN concurrently ,we simply created a list of Callable and THEN we have forked them using fork(..) , we are joining then using join() method.

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.concurrent.Callable;
import java.util.concurrent.StructuredTaskScope; import java.util.concurrent.StructuredTaskScope.*;

public class StructuredConcurrencyDemo3 {
public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception { Callable<String> task1 = ()-> "task1";
Callable<String> task2 = ()-> "task2";
Callable<String> task3 = ()-> "task3";
Callable<String> task4 = ()-> "task4";

List<Callable> tasks= new ArrayList<>(); tasks.add(task1);
tasks.add(task2);
tasks.add(task3);
tasks.add(task4);

try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope<String>()) {

List<Subtask> subtasks = tasks
.stream()
.map(x->scope.fork(x))

.toList();
scope.join();
subtasks.forEach(x->System.out.println(x.get()+ " :: "+x.state())); }
}
}
Result:

task1 :: SUCCESS task2 :: SUCCESS task3 :: SUCCESS task4 :: SUCCESS

As we can see here, we are using the default public constructor of
StructuredTaskScope which will create virtual threads internally to complete these tasks.we are simply waiting for all the tasks to complete here BUT we also may have the option to exit if any of the tasks is failed.

StructuredTaskScope policy

In the example above, we waited for all tasks to complete using
StructuredTaskScope.join(). But if an exception occurs in one of the tasks, we can’t do anything with the results of the other two tasks– so why wait for them?

This is where the socalled “policies” come into picture, allowing us to specify when a scope is finished, among other things. StructuredTaskScope defines two such policies BUT we can create our own policies also if required.

Two subclasses of StructuredTaskScope are defined to implement policy for common cases.
1. ShutdownOnSuccessPREVIEW 2. ShutdownOnFailurePREVIEW
ShutdownOnFailure :
If any of the subtask fails due to any reason then an exception will be captured and it shuts down the task scope to interrupt unfinished threads.

As you can see the below code, we are calling join() method and THEN calling throwIfFailed() so if any subtask fails then the results of other unfinished subtasks are no

longer needed
import java.util.concurrent.Callable;
import java.util.concurrent.StructuredTaskScope;

public class StructuredConcurrencyDemo1 {
public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception { Callable<String> task1 = ()-> "Welcome";
Callable<Integer> task2 = ()-> 12;

try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope.ShutdownOnFailure()) {
StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<String> subtask1 = scope.fork(task1); StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<Integer> subtask2 = scope.fork(task2);
scope.join()
.throwIfFailed(); //process results/exceptions
}

}
}
NOTE: Here both tasks are successfully done without any error so NO Interruption will be done.

Let's have a look into below code where task2 is failing import java.util.concurrent.Callable;
import java.util.concurrent.StructuredTaskScope;

public class StructuredConcurrencyDemo1 {
public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception { Callable<String> task1 = ()-> "Welcome";
Callable<Integer> task2 = ()-> 12/0;

try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope.ShutdownOnFailure()) {
StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<String> subtask1 = scope.fork(task1); StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<Integer> subtask2 = scope.fork(task2);

scope.join()
.throwIfFailed();
//process results/exceptions }
}
}

ShutdownOnSuccess:
ShutdownOnSuccess captures the result of the first subtask to complete successfully. Once captured, it shuts down the task scope to interrupt unfinished threads and wake up the owner.
This class is intended for cases where the result of any subtask will do the needful and where there is no need to wait for results of other unfinished subtasks.
It defines methods to get the first result or throw an exception if all subtasks fail.
import java.util.concurrent.Callable;
import java.util.concurrent.StructuredTaskScope;

public class StructuredConcurrencyDemo2 {
public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception { Callable<String> task1 = ()-> "Welcome";
Callable<Integer> task2 = ()-> 12/0;

try (var scope = new StructuredTaskScope.ShutdownOnSuccess<>()) {
StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<String> subtask1 = scope.fork(task1); StructuredTaskScope.Subtask<Integer> subtask2 = scope.fork(task2); scope.join();
//process results/exceptions }
}
}

String Template

String Templates greatly improves how we create strings in Java by merging constant strings with variable values.
String Template is something like patterns that include String literals and variable values.
In a simple way, we can say that it's a simplified way to create String in java.
Benefits of String Template:

●Simplify the process to express Strings with values that can be compiled at run time. ●Enhanced readability of String
●String can simply be written without a value just with a placeholder and it can be

provided at runtime.this will increase security also.

Template Expression:

It's a programmable Java expression. Programmable template expressions can perform interpolation and also provide us with the flexibility to compose the Strings safely and efficiently in a more simplified way.
There are three components to a template expression:

●A processor
●A template which contains the data/text with expressions
●A dot (.) character

The Template Processor helps to evaluate Template expression and combine with String literals at runtime.
Java does provides set of Template processor that you can use Java provides the ability to use You can also create your own Template Processor after Java 21 since its a preview feature in Java 21. Lets look into Java in-build Template Processors:

STR Template Processor:

The STR Template Processor performs string interpolation by replacing each embedded expression of the provided template with the value of that expression.
public class StringTemplateDemo {
public static String formatString(String name, String programName) {
return STR
. "Welcome to PalywithJava,Mr. \{name}, We will start \{programName}" ; }
public static String formatString2(String name, String programName) {
return STR
. "Welcome to PalywithJava,Mr. \{name} \nWe will start \{programName}" ; }

public static void main(String[] args) { String strDemo1=formatString("Raj","Java"); String strDemo2=formatString2("Raj","Java");

System.out.println(" String Demo1: "+strDemo1); System.out.println(" String Demo2: "+strDemo2); }
}
Result:
String Demo1: Welcome to PalywithJava,Mr. Raj, We will start
String Demo2: Welcome to PalywithJava,Mr. Raj We will start
STR Process can even be used with Multi-Line String also.
public class StringTemplateSTRDemo { public static void main(String[] args) {

In this course we will learn java 21 feature We will also learn about other java versions """;

System.out.println("Addr : "+addr); }
}
Result:

Addr : Hi, Welcome to Java 21
In this course we will learn java 21 feature We will also learn about other java versions

<head>

<title>\{ title}</title> </head>
<body>

<p>\{ text}</p>
</body>
</html>
""";
System.out.println(webpage); }

Let's generate Table view using STR Processor:
public class FmtProcessorDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {

var item1="Item1"; var width1=10;
var hight1=22;
var aria1=220;

var item2="Item1"; var width2=5;
var hight2=22;
var aria2=110;

var total=330;

String table = STR."""
Description\tWidth\tHeight\tAria
\{item1}\t\t\{width1}\t\t\{hight1}\t\t\{width1*hight1} \{item2}\t\t\{width2}\t\t\{hight2}\t\t\{width2*hight2} \{getBorder()}
Total \{aria1 + aria2}
\{getBorder()}
""";
System.out.println(table);
}

public static StringBuffer getBorder(){ return new StringBuffer().repeat("*",50);
}
}

Item1 10 22 220
Item1 5 22 110

FMT Processors

FTM Processor is mainly used to format the String data as per the required formatting style.
import static java.util.FormatProcessor.FMT;
public class StringTemplateDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {

var name="Raj";
var rating=2.5;
var mob="9876567811";

String data= FMT. """
{
"Name": "%1s\{name}",
"Address": "%2.9f\{rating}", "Mob": "%10s\{ mob }"
}
""" ;

System.out.println(data); }
}
Result:

{
"Name": "Raj",
"Address": "2.500000000", "Mob": "9876567811" }

Pattern Matching With Switch Case

Pattern matching for switch was first previewed in JDK 17 and was subsequently refined in JDK 18, JDK 19, and JDK 20. The feature is now finalized and will be included in JDK 21.
Lets understand Pattern Matching first:

The technique of pattern matching is about testing an expression against certain characteristics. A variant of pattern matching you’re certainly familiar with is type pattern matching with theinstanceof keyword:

if (anyObj instanceof String) {
var str = (String) anyObj;
...

}

Matching the pattern alone is only half the work, usually, we still want to use the value as the actual type. That means we still need to cast the pattern-verified variable. Thanks to Java 16 and JEP 394, though, type pattern matching got a little easier:

// As of Java 16
if (anyObj instanceof String str) { ...
}

There’s no longer a need to declare a new variable and cast the original value after matching! That’s a great improvement, but how about checking against multiple patterns?

So far, the only option was using as manyif-else as needed:
static String asStringValue(Object anyValue) { String result = "n/a";

if (anyValue instanceof String str) {
result = str;
} else if (anyValue instanceof JSONObject json) { result = json.toCompactString();
} else if (anyValue instanceof BigDecimal bd) { result = bd.toEngineeringString();
} else if (anyValue instance Integer i) {
result = Integer.toString(i);
} else if (anyValue instanceof LocalDate ld) { result = ld.format(DateTimeFormatter.ISO_LOCAL_DATE);
} else {
// default case
}

return result; }

Pattern Matching

Looking at how the switch works so far, it really simplifies the use-case where we used to have multiple if-else conditions.
The next logical evolution would be to allow patterns to match values in a switch, not only constants. You can even write a Switch case with Return

switch + instanceof

Like aninstanceof in anif condition, a switch case can now type-check its value and creates a case-scoped variable:
The main purpose of this feature is to simplify the switch statements by allowing patterns to appear in case labels.
This will help to address one of the main limitations of classic switch statements, only being able to switch on primitive numeric, string, and enum.
Lets understand with simple Example:
public String getAnimalSound(Animal animal) { if (animal == null) {
System.out.println("Oops! A null animal?");

} else if (animal instanceof Dog) { Dog dog = (Dog) animal;
return dog.bark();

} else if (animal instanceof Cat) { Cat cat = (Cat) animal;
return cat.meow();

} else if (animal instanceof Bird) { Bird bird = (Bird) animal; return bird.chirp();

}
}

As you can see in the above example, this would be an excellent use case for switch statements as there are several if-else statements that need to be simplified. But prior to Java 21, type limitations on case labels would prevent us from being able to use switch statements here but now the same piece of code can be simplified as below.

public String getAnimalSound(Animal animal) {
switch (animal) {
case null -> System.out.println("Ohh! Invalid option"); case Dog dog -> return dog.bark();
case Cat cat -> return cat.meow();
case Bird bird -> return bird.chirp();

}
}
Example 2:
static String asStringValue(Object anyValue) {
return switch (anyValue) {
case String str -> str;
JSONObject json -> json.toCompactString(); case

case BigDecimal bd case Integer i case LocalDate ld default

};
}

-> bd.toEngineeringString();
-> Integer.toString(i);
-> ld.format(DateTimeFormatter.ISO_LOCAL_DATE);
-> "n/a";

Another enhancement : the ability to switch on null, as previously a null check would have to be handled outside of the switch statement. Being able to handle the null case inside our switch makes our code safer, cleaner, and more concise.

We have already discussed about Switch case evolution from Java 7 to Java 20 in later section of this book (as part of Java 17 Features)

// Prior to Java 21
static String formatter(Object obj) { String formatted = "unknown"; if (obj instanceof Integer i) {

formatted = String.format("int %d", i); } else if (obj instanceof Long l) {
formatted = String.format("long %d", l); } else if (obj instanceof Double d) {
formatted = String.format("double %f", d); } else if (obj instanceof String s) {
formatted = String.format("String %s", s); }
return formatted;

}
// As of Java 21
static String formatterPatternSwitch(Object obj) { return switch (obj) {

case Integer i -> String.format("int %d", i); case Long l -> String.format("long %d", l); case Double d -> String.format("double %f", d); case String s -> String.format("String %s", s); case null > “NA”
default -> obj.toString();

};
}

import java.math.BigDecimal; import java.time.LocalDate; import java.time.LocalDateTime; public class Java21SwitchDemo {

static void asStringValue(Object anyValue) { switch (anyValue) { case String str case BigDecimal bd case Integer i case LocalDate ld
-> System.out.println("String :: "+str);
-> System.out.println("BigDecimal : "+bd.toEngineeringString());
-> System.out.println("Integer ::"+i);
-> System.out.println("Local Date :: "+ld.toString());

case LocalDateTime ld -> System.out.println("Local date Time :: "+ld.toString()); case null -> System.out.println("You have provided NULL value");
default -> System.out.println(" Invalid Switch option");

};
}

public static void main(String[] args) { asStringValue(Integer.valueOf(11)); asStringValue(LocalDate.now()); asStringValue(LocalDateTime.now()); asStringValue(null);

}
}

Result:

Integer ::11
Local Date :: 2024-03-21
Local date Time :: 2024-03-21T15:33:44.609907 You have provided NULL value

switch + null

As we know, While using multiple “if-else” or Switch Statements, We must do a NULL check else it will throw a NullPointerException.provided null check is again not a good practice since we have many conditions.

With Java 21,switch finally allowscase null to be included:

static String asStringValue(Object anyValue) { return switch (anyValue) {
case null -> "n/a";
case String str -> str;

};
}
Example:
// Prior to Java 21
static void testFooBarOld(String s) {
if (s == null) {

System. out.println("Oops!");
return;
}
switch (s) {
case "Foo", "Bar" -> System.out.println("Great"); default -> System.out.println("Ok");
}}

// As of Java 21
static void testFooBarNew(String s) {
switch (s) {

case null -> System.out.println("Oops"); case "Foo", "Bar" -> System.out.println("Great"); default -> System.out.println("Ok");

}}
// As of Java 21
static void testStringOld(String response) { switch (response) {
case null -> { }
case String s -> {
if (s.equalsIgnoreCase("YES"))

System. out.println("You got it"); else if (s.equalsIgnoreCase("NO"))
System.out.println("Shame"); else
System.out.println("Sorry?"); }}}

// As of Java 21
static void testStringNew(String response) { switch (response) {
case null -> { }
case String s
when s.equalsIgnoreCase("YES") -> {

System. out.println("You got it"); }
case String s

when s.equalsIgnoreCase("NO") -> {

System. out.println("Shame");
}
case String s -> {

System.out.println("Sorry?"); }}}

Multi Case:

If a value has more than one value, we can already test against it by “falling through” cases:

static boolean itsTheWeekend(DayOfWeek dow) { return switch(dow) {
case SATURDAY:
case SUNDAY:

yield true;
default:
yield false; };
}
You can write comma separated CASE values as below.

static boolean itsTheWeekend(DayOfWeek dow) { return switch(dow) {
case SATURDAY, SUNDAY -> true;
default -> false;

};
}

static String describe(DayOfWeek dow) { return switch(dow) { case TUESDAY

case WEDNESDAY

-> "Taco Tuesday";
-> "Margarita Wednesday"; case SATURDAY, SUNDAY -> "Party time!"; default -> "Boring work day...";

};
}
Handle default case and NULL together with the same switch value.

static String asStringValue(Object anyValue) {
return switch (anyValue) {
case null, default -> "n/a";
case String str -> str;

};
}

Guard Clauses

If we need to check for multiple conditions in the passed value in the switch we can use a guarded pattern. Guarded patterns avoid the need of additional if blocks in the switch and we can use the conditional logic in the case labels.

In the below program we are using a guarded pattern to check and parse a float value.

static void test(Object obj) {
switch (obj) {
case String s when s.length()== 1->System.out.println("Short: "+s); case String s -> System.out.println(s);
default -> System.out.println("Not a string");

}
}

Prior to Java 21

// Prior to Java 21
public enum Suit { CLUBS, DIAMONDS, HEARTS, SPADES }

static void testforHearts(Suit s) {
switch (s) {
case HEARTS -> System.out.println("It's a heart!"); default -> System.out.println("Some other suit");

}}

As of Java 21

sealed interface CardClassification permits Suit, Tarot {}
public enum Suit implements CardClassification { CLUBS, DIAMONDS, HEARTS, SPADES }
final class Tarot implements CardClassification {}
public class SwitchDemo {
static void exhaustiveSwitchWithoutEnumSupport(CardClassification c) { switch (c) {
case Suit s when s == Suit.CLUBS -> {

System. out.println("It's clubs");
}
case Suit s when s == Suit.DIAMONDS -> {

System. out.println("It's diamonds");
}
case Suit s when s == Suit.HEARTS -> {

System. out.println("It's hearts"); }
case Suit s -> {

System. out.println("It's spades"); }
case Tarot t -> {

System.out.println("It's a tarot"); }
}
}

public static void main(String[] args) {
exhaustiveSwitchWithoutEnumSupport(Suit.CLUBS);
}
}
Result: It's clubs

Let's do the same in more flexible way:
// As of Java 21
static void exhaustiveSwitchWithBetterEnumSupport(CardClassification c) { switch (c) {
case Suit.CLUBS -> {

System.out.println("It's clubs");
}
case Suit.DIAMONDS -> {

System.out.println("It's diamonds");
}
case Suit.HEARTS -> {

System.out.println("It's hearts");
}
case Suit.SPADES -> {

System.out.println("It's spades");
}
case Tarot t -> {

System.out.println("It's a tarot");
}
}
}

Switches and enum constants
The use of enum constants in case labels is, at present, highly constrained: The selector expression of the switch must be of the enum type, and the labels must be simple names of the enum's constants. For example:

// Prior to Java 21
public enum Suit { CLUBS, DIAMONDS, HEARTS, SPADES }

static void testforHearts(Suit s) {
switch (s) {
case HEARTS -> System.out.println("It's a heart!"); default -> System.out.println("Some other suit");

}
// As of Java 21

sealed interface CardClassification permits Suit, Tarot {}
public enum Suit implements CardClassification { CLUBS, DIAMONDS, HEARTS, SPADES } final class Tarot implements CardClassification {}

static void exhaustiveSwitchWithoutEnumSupport(CardClassification c) { switch (c) {
case Suit s when s == Suit.CLUBS -> {

System.out.println("It's clubs");
}
case Suit s when s == Suit.DIAMONDS -> {

System.out.println("It's diamonds");
}
case Suit s when s == Suit.HEARTS -> {

System.out.println("It's hearts");
}
case Suit s -> {

System.out.println("It's spades");
}
case Tarot t -> {

System.out.println("It's a tarot");
}
}
}
// As of Java 21
static void exhaustiveSwitchWithBetterEnumSupport(CardClassification c) { switch (c) {
case Suit.CLUBS -> {

System.out.println("It's clubs");
}
case Suit.DIAMONDS -> {

System.out.println("It's diamonds");
}
case Suit.HEARTS -> {

System.out.println("It's hearts");
}
case Suit.SPADES -> {

System.out.println("It's spades");
}
case Tarot t -> {

System.out.println("It's a tarot");
}
}
Example 2: Java 16
record Point(int x, int y) {}

static void printSum(Object obj) { if (obj instanceof Point p) { int x = p.x();
int y = p.y();
System.out.println(x+y);

}
}
// As of Java 21
static void printSum(Object obj) { if (obj instanceof Point(int x, int y)) { System.out.println(x+y);
}
}

Record class Enhancements

We have already discussed about Record classes in section ‘Java 17 features’ , you can simply visit
Lets understand what has been simplified using record class as part of java 21.
As we know, we used to have a Bean/POJO class as a data carrier class in application.
When we create a POJO class for data carrier class then we used to implement hashCode() and equals(...) method with required params so that we can simply equate two Java objects.
Lets understand it with Example:
Create POJO Class: class Employee{

String name; Integer empID; String city;

public Employee(String name, Integer empID, String city) { this.name = name;
this.empID = empID;
this.city = city;

}
}
Test Class
public class RecordClassDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {
Employee e1=new Employee("Raj",11,"Bangalore"); Employee e2=new Employee("Raj",11,"Bangalore");
System.out.println(" e1 equals e2 :"+(e1.equals(e2))); }
}
Result:
e1 equals e2 :false

As you can see that we have 2 Employee objects e1 and e2 and both have the same content also BUT still its NOT equal because default implementation for this equal(..) methods happens based on reference NOT based on content.

As you can see the Result is false.
Let's look at its implementation:
Normally it's mandatory to implement hashCode() as per java specification ,for more info you can check API docs.
Here if you want the comparison to happen based on content you must override the behavior of this equals(..) method and hashCode() method.
import java.util.Objects;

class Employee{ String name; Integer empID; String city;

public Employee(String name, Integer empID, String city) { this.name = name;
this.empID = empID;
this.city = city;

}

@Override
public boolean equals(Object o) {
if (this == o) return true;
if (o == null || getClass() != o.getClass()) return false;
Employee employee = (Employee) o;
return Objects.equals(name, employee.name) && Objects.equals(empID, employee.empID) && Objects.equals(city, employee.city);
}

@Override
public int hashCode() {
return Objects.hash(name, empID, city); }
}
public class RecordClassDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {
Employee e1=new Employee("Raj",11,"Bangalore"); Employee e2=new Employee("Raj",11,"Bangalore");
System.out.println(" e1 equals e2 :"+(e1.equals(e2))); }
}
Result:
e1 equals e2 :true
As you can see here, for POJO class we must need to override these methods THEN only we can equate 2 java objects based on contents.

We can simplify it using Record class.

Simplifying it using Record Class
public class RecordClassDemo {
public static void main(String[] args) {
Employee e1=new Employee("Raj",11,"Bangalore"); Employee e2=new Employee("Raj",11,"Bangalore");
System.out.println(" e1 equals e2 :"+(e1.equals(e2))); }
}
record Employee( String name,Integer empID,String city){}; Result:

e1 equals e2 :true
● Here Java compiler happily generates bytecode for us, taking into account all parameters defined to implement equals and hashCode methods. ● Records classes also provide toString() and getter methods, but no setters. ● Record classes are designed to be immutable, which means once an instance is created we can only read object members and cannot change their values.

Record patterns were first previewed in JDK 19, and were subsequently refined in JDK 20. The feature is now ready for release and will be included in JDK 21!
The goal of this new feature is to extend pattern matching to records, allowing for more sophisticated data queries.

Create Record and Interface as below:

record Dog(String dogBreed, String dogName) implements Animal {} record Cat(String catBreed, String catName) implements Animal {} interface Animal{}
public class RecordWithPatterDemo {

public static void getDogDetails(Animal animal) {
if (animal instanceof Dog) {
Dog dog = (Dog) animal;
String dogBreed = dog.dogBreed();
String dogName = dog.dogName();
System.out.println(dogName+" the "+dogBreed+" is such a good Dog !!");

}
}
public static void main(String[] args) {
Dog dog=new Dog("Bulldog","Moti"); getDogDetails(dog);
}
}
Result:
Moti the Bulldog is such a good dog!
Here we have record classes for Dog and Cat with the fields name and breed.

Prior to Java 21, if we wanted to print out the details for an animal, specifically a dog/cat, we would have to check the object type, cast it, and define new variables to extract the fields we want as you can see in the above example.

public class RecordWithPatterDemo {
public static void getDogDetailsWithJava21(Animal animal) { if (animal instanceof Dog(String dogBreed, String dogName)) {

System. out.println(dogName+" the "+dogBreed+" is such a good dog!"); }
}
public static void main(String[] args) {

Dog dog=new Dog("Bulldog","Moti");
getDogDetailsWithJava21(dog);
}
}

Result: Moti the Bulldog is such a good dog!
